# AI coding assistant transcript

**Assistant:** Claude Code (Anthropic), used in the VS Code extension for the whole project.
**Period:** 2026-10-26T11:07:09.500Z to 2026-10-27T09:46:19.516Z (UTC), one continuous session.

## How this file was produced

- **Source:** generated by a script from Claude Code's own session log. It is not Claude Code's `/export` output.
- **Copied verbatim:** every user prompt and every assistant reply, apart from redaction. Nothing was paraphrased,
  summarised or added by hand.
- **Shown as one-line markers:** tool calls (file reads and edits, shell commands, searches), each with its tool name
  and stated purpose, under "Tool calls".
- **Left out:**
  - tool outputs (command results and file contents);
  - the assistant's internal reasoning;
  - automatic context notes and environment notices inserted by Claude Code, which the user did not write;
  - log metadata.
- **Compaction:** the conversation was compacted 2 times. Each Claude Code summary appears where
  it happened and is labelled; the messages before it are still included in full.
- **Redacted:** local home-directory and temporary paths, the session ID, email addresses, anything shaped like an
  API key (including the deliberately fake keys used in tests), the account holder's name, and the name of an
  unrelated project.
- **Not included:** no `.env` contents were ever printed in the session; only provider and model names were read.

**Counts:** 57 user messages, 240 assistant replies, 935 tool calls,
2 compaction summaries.

### A. Files created or changed
The project folder was empty and not a Git repository, so no existing files were overwritten. Everything below is new:
- **Config and docs:** [README.md](README.md), [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md), [.gitignore](.gitignore), [.env.example](.env.example), [pyproject.toml](pyproject.toml), [requirements.txt](requirements.txt), [requirements-dev.txt](requirements-dev.txt), [data/README.md](data/README.md)
- **App:** [app/main.py](app/main.py), [app/core/config.py](app/core/config.py), [app/core/logging.py](app/core/logging.py), [app/api/routes/health.py](app/api/routes/health.py), [app/schemas/health.py](app/schemas/health.py), plus empty package files in `agents/`, `tools/`, `orchestration/`, `evaluation/`, `api/`, `core/` and `schemas/`
- **Tests:** [tests/conftest.py](tests/conftest.py), [tests/test_health.py](tests/test_health.py)
- **Local only:** a `.venv/` folder (Python 3.13.7) and a fresh Git repo. Both `.venv/` and `.env` are ignored by Git. **No commit was made.**

### B. Architecture and configuration decisions
- **Structure:** one `app/` package, so imports work the same in every later phase.
- **Startup:** `create_app()` builds the application, and `app` is the entry point uvicorn runs.
- **Settings:** settings come from `.env` or environment variables, loaded with `pydantic-settings`.
  - `LLM_API_KEY` is typed so that printing or logging it shows `**********` instead of the value.
  - The key is optional for now and nothing reads it yet.
  - `.env.example` names the AI provider only generically, because no provider has been chosen.
- **Health endpoint:** it receives settings in a way tests can override. It returns only `status`, `app_name`, `version` and `environment`, never configuration values.
- **Tests:** tests ignore any local `.env` file. One test plants a fake key and checks it never appears in the `/health` response or in printed settings.
- **Dependencies:** runtime is `fastapi`, `uvicorn[standard]` and `pydantic-settings`; testing adds `pytest` and `httpx`. Versions are allowed within a range rather than fixed. There is no agent framework and no frontend.

## User

[pasted text]

We are starting Phase 1 of the Orange Invest AI Engineer assignment: Incident Schemas and Evidence Tools.

Phase 0 is reported complete: FastAPI starts, GET /health returns HTTP 200 with JSON, and four tests pass. The development environment is macOS with zsh and Python 3.13.7. Do not assume Windows PowerShell.

Before coding, inspect the existing project files, configuration, tests, and Git status. Preserve the working Phase 0 foundation. If you find a real issue in Phase 0, report it and make only a minimal, justified fix.

## Objective

Build the typed data models and deterministic, locally runnable evidence tools that future investigation agents will use.

Do not implement the agents or full orchestration yet. Do not add a frontend or make real OpenAI API calls in this phase.

## 1. Define structured schemas

Use Pydantic models consistent with the existing project conventions for:

- Incident report: incident ID, summary, affected services, severity, reported time, and available context.
- Evidence result: evidence source, query, returned records, completeness, warnings, and errors where appropriate.
- Log records.
- Performance metric records.
- Deployment/configuration history records.
- Service dependency records.
- Historical incident records.

Use sensible types, validation, timestamps, and clear field names. Avoid adding unnecessary required fields that make incomplete incident reports impossible to represent. Do not silently convert missing evidence into proof that an event did not happen.

## 2. Implement five deterministic evidence tools

Create modular tools for:

1. Searching logs.
2. Querying performance metrics.
3. Retrieving deployment/configuration history.
4. Retrieving service dependency information.
5. Searching historical incidents.

Use local synthetic JSON or JSONL fixtures. Each tool must have a clear input schema and output schema, predictable filtering, and useful error handling.

The tools should support relevant filters such as incident time range, service name, severity, metric name, deployment time, or incident keywords, depending on the tool.

Do not hardcode the final answer or root cause for any individual incident. The tools should return evidence, not conclusions.

## 3. Handle imperfect evidence

Design a simple, testable way to represent:

- No matching records.
- Partial or incomplete results.
- Malformed fixture data where practical.
- Simulated tool timeouts or failures.

Keep failure simulation deterministic and controllable in tests. Do not add arbitrary sleeps or rely on real external services. Do not pretend a simulated failure is a real network failure.

## 4. Synthetic data

Create small, understandable fixtures covering multiple services and timestamps, including at least one example of:
- A deployment near an incident.
- Performance degradation.
- Logs that suggest a possible issue without proving the root cause.
- Missing or conflicting evidence.

The same fixture data must be usable by the later multi-agent system and single-agent baseline.

## 5. Tests

Add pytest coverage for:
- Schema validation and invalid inputs.
- Correct tool filtering and structured outputs.
- Empty results.
- Partial evidence.
- Simulated tool failures.
- Malformed data handling, if supported.

Keep tests deterministic and independent of any real API key or paid LLM call.

Run the complete existing test suite, not only new tests. Fix regressions before reporting completion.

## 6. Scope and safety

- Preserve the existing health endpoint and configuration.
- Keep secrets out of fixtures, responses, and logs.
- Do not add an agent framework, database, frontend, or real LLM integration yet.
- Keep dependencies minimal; justify any new dependency.
- Do not rewrite unrelated files.
- Do not claim that a test or command passed unless you actually ran it.

## Acceptance criteria

Phase 1 is ready for review only if:
- All five tools exist and have documented input/output contracts.
- Synthetic evidence is stored locally and is reusable.
- Empty, partial, and simulated failure cases are handled explicitly.
- Tests verify normal and failure behavior.
- The entire test suite passes.
- README or relevant documentation explains how the tools and fixtures work.

## Final report

Stop after Phase 1 and report:

A. Files created or changed.
B. Schema and tool design decisions, including why.
C. Example tool inputs and outputs.
D. Commands actually executed.
E. Exact test results and any warnings.
F. Known limitations and anything not verified.
G. A recommendation: GO, GO WITH FIXES, or NO-GO.

Wait for my review before starting Phase 2.

### A. Files created or changed

- **The five tools:** [app/tools/logs.py](app/tools/logs.py), [app/tools/metrics.py](app/tools/metrics.py), [app/tools/changes.py](app/tools/changes.py), [app/tools/dependencies.py](app/tools/dependencies.py), [app/tools/historical_incidents.py](app/tools/historical_incidents.py)
- **Fixtures:** 7 JSONL files in [data/fixtures/](data/fixtures/), 303 records in total

- [tests/conftest.py](tests/conftest.py): added two fixtures; the existing ones are untouched.

Nothing else changed, and there are no new dependencies.

### B. Design decisions and why
- **One result shape for every tool.** Each tool returns an `EvidenceResult` with a derived `status` of `ok`, `no_matches`, `partial` or `failed`. This keeps "found nothing" and "could not look" distinct, and the model refuses inconsistent combinations, such as a failed result that contains records.
- **An empty result is never treated as proof.** Every empty result carries a warning that the absence of records does not show the event didn't happen.
- **Known data gaps are declared in data.** A small file lists windows where a source has no data, for example missing fraud-check metrics. Any query overlapping a gap comes back `partial` with the reason attached. A query with no service filter covers all services, so it picks up every gap. That caused 3 of my test queries to fail at first; I kept the behaviour and narrowed those queries.
- **Tools don't raise for expected problems.**
  - Bad input, a missing dataset or an injected fault each come back as a structured error with a code, a retryable flag and a `simulated` flag.
  - Validation messages give field names, not values, so fixture contents don't end up in logs.
- **Failure simulation is fixed per tool name.** It never sleeps or touches the network, and the error says it was injected.
- **Incomplete reports are allowed.** An incident report needs only an ID and a summary; a missing value means "not provided".
- **Strict input checking.** Unknown fields are rejected and timestamps must include a timezone, which catches typos in fixtures and in future agent tool calls.
- **Agent-ready.** Each tool exposes its name, a description and a JSON schema for its input, ready for Phase 2 tool calling.
- **Naming:** the deployment/configuration tool is called `get_change_history`, because it also covers config changes, feature flags and rollbacks.

### C. Example inputs and outputs (real output, trimmed)
- **Normal result:** `get_change_history` with `{start: 2026-10-20T13:30Z, end: 15:00Z, services: [payments-api]}` returns `status: ok` with 2 records: CHG-3101 (a deploy at 13:52) and CHG-3102 (a config change at 13:55 lowering `db.pool.max_size` from 50 to 20).
- **Missing evidence:** `query_metrics` for fraud-check in the same window returns `status: partial` with 0 records and two warnings: "Known coverage gap … Vendor metrics export delayed …" and the note that absence is not proof.
- **Simulated timeout** on `search_logs`: `status: failed`, error `{code: timeout, retryable: true, simulated: true, message: "Simulated timeout injected by the fault plan; no external call was made."}`.
- **Invalid query** (end before start): `status: failed`, error `{code: invalid_query, message: "Invalid query: (root): Value error, end must be after start", retryable: false}`.

### D. Commands actually executed
1. `git status`, a file listing and a baseline `pytest -q`: 4 passed, 1 warning, no commits yet.
2. A generator script, kept outside the repo, wrote `data/fixtures/*.jsonl`. I then loaded every dataset through the fixture loader: 0 issues.
3. `pytest -q`: 3 failed, 80 passed (the test-query issue above). After narrowing those queries and adding one test for the gap behaviour: 84 passed.
4. Ran the tools from Python to capture the outputs in C, and ran the README example (`ok 2`).
5. Started uvicorn and called `/health`: HTTP 200 with the same JSON as Phase 0, then stopped the server.
6. A secret scan: the only matches are the `llm_api_key` type declaration and the fake test value.

### E. Exact test results
`.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q` gave **`84 passed, 1 warning in 0.06s`**. The one warning is the same as Phase 0: Starlette's `httpx` deprecation notice, which doesn't affect results.

### F. Known limitations and what was not verified
- **Gaps are stored with fixed dates.** A gap's dates are fixed, so the year-long "fraud-check change history not visible" gap marks every unfiltered change query in 2026 as `partial`. That is deliberate and documented, but it may be noisy for agents.
- **Simple matching.** Keyword search is plain substring matching (so "pool" also matches "spool") and historical search ranks by keyword count only. Both are predictable but crude.
- **Failure simulation is all-or-nothing.** There is no "fail on the Nth call", so testing retries in Phase 2 may need it.
- **Load-time problems are only partly covered.** Malformed lines and missing files are tested; an unreadable file (permissions or encoding) is handled in code but not tested.
- **No checks on the data's story.** Tests verify the fixtures are valid, not that the scenarios stay ambiguous; that rests on review of [data/README.md](data/README.md).
- **Phase 0 issue, reported but not fixed:** `app.main` builds the app when it is imported, so a malformed local `.env` (for example an invalid `APP_ENV`) would stop the tests from loading. Tests don't depend on `.env` values otherwise.
- The README setup section still leads with Windows PowerShell; the macOS commands are further down. I left it as is to avoid rewriting unrelated files.
- Not verified: Python 3.12 (only 3.13.7 was run), and the generator script that made the fixtures isn't in the repo, so the JSONL files are the source of truth.
- Nothing is committed yet.

### G. Recommendation: **GO**
All five tools exist with documented contracts. Empty, partial, malformed and simulated-failure cases are each handled and tested, and the entire suite passes. I've stopped here and won't start Phase 2 until you've reviewed it.

## A. GitHub readiness and safety

1. Inspect `git status`, the current branch, existing commits, and configured remotes.
2. Inspect `.gitignore` and verify `.env`, `.venv/`, Python caches, test caches, and generated artifacts are excluded as appropriate.
3. Check whether `.env` or other likely secret files are already tracked. Do not print actual secret values.
4. Check staged and unstaged changes before any commit.
5. If GitHub CLI (`gh`) is installed, check authentication status without displaying credentials. Do not install it automatically.
6. Do not create a public repository. Prefer a private repository for this assignment.
7. Do not create a remote repository, commit, push, overwrite a remote, or rewrite Git history without first reporting what you found and obtaining confirmation for any action that could publish or overwrite data.
8. If a GitHub remote already exists, report its URL and whether the working tree is clean. Redact credentials if a URL contains them.
9. If no remote exists, prepare the repository for a private GitHub remote and tell me the exact next steps needed. Do not invent a repository URL.
10. Never expose API keys, access tokens, or `.env` contents in reports, terminal output, or commits.

## B. Minimal Phase 1.1 code/documentation review

1. Make the macOS/zsh setup instructions the primary setup path in README.md. Keep Windows PowerShell instructions in a separate section.
2. Review the reported configuration issue: importing `app.main` constructs the app, and malformed `.env` values may cause tests to fail during import.
3. Determine whether this issue is real by inspecting the code and writing a focused test where practical.
4. If a minimal, safe fix is appropriate, implement it without weakening configuration validation or hiding real configuration errors. Do not silently fall back to insecure defaults.
5. Preserve the health endpoint, all five evidence tools, existing schemas, and fixture behavior.
6. Do not modify unrelated architecture, introduce new dependencies, or change the existing synthetic incident data.
7. Do not upgrade dependencies just to remove the existing Starlette/httpx warning.

## C. Verification

Run the complete test suite and report the exact result. Also run `git diff --check` and inspect the final diff. Check for accidental secret files and make sure the changes are limited to this phase.

## Final report

Report:
- Git branch, status, commits, and remote status.
- Whether GitHub CLI is installed and authenticated, without revealing credentials.
- Secret-hygiene findings, without printing secret values.
- Files changed and why.
- Whether the malformed-configuration issue was reproduced and how it was addressed.

We are starting Phase 2 of the Orange Invest AI Engineer assignment: the Multi-Agent Orchestration Engine.

Current project status:
- Phase 0–1.1 has been committed and pushed to GitHub.
- The previous full test suite had 91 passing tests and one existing warning.
- Five deterministic evidence tools and structured schemas already exist.
- Development environment: macOS, zsh, Python 3.13.7.
- Do not assume any uncommitted changes are safe to overwrite.

## First: inspect before implementing

1. Inspect `git status`, current branch, latest commit, project structure, evidence tool contracts, and existing tests.
2. Run the full existing test suite and report the baseline.
3. Stop and report if the working tree contains unexplained changes or if the baseline tests fail. Do not discard user changes.
4. Read `docs/TOOLS.md` and the existing schemas before designing the orchestration interface.

## Objective

Implement a reliable, deterministic orchestration engine that can coordinate investigation tasks. This phase must not implement the six LLM agents yet, and must not make real OpenAI API calls.

## Requirements

### 1. Shared investigation state

Create typed models for:
- Investigation ID and incident input.
- Task status and task results.
- Evidence results returned by the existing tools.
- Task attempts, errors, and timing information where appropriate.
- Overall investigation status and termination reason.

Keep state transitions explicit and validated. Preserve evidence provenance. Distinguish missing, partial, failed, and successful evidence; never treat a failed query as evidence that no event occurred.

### 2. Task planning and dependencies

Implement a small task model with:
- Unique task ID.
- Task type/name.
- Declared dependencies.
- Input data or references to prior results.
- Retry policy and timeout.
- Current status and structured output.

Reject dependency cycles and unknown dependency IDs. Tasks must not execute before their dependencies succeed or reach a clearly defined handled-failure state.

### 3. Parallel execution

Independent evidence tasks should be able to execute concurrently using Python's standard async facilities where appropriate.

Add deterministic tests showing:
- Independent tasks can run concurrently.
- A dependent task waits for its prerequisites.
- Results are recorded correctly.
- One task's failure does not silently corrupt unrelated results.

Avoid arbitrary sleeps in tests where synchronization primitives can demonstrate concurrency more reliably.

### 4. Bounded retries and timeouts

Implement configurable maximum attempts and task timeouts.

- Retry only failures classified as retryable.
- Do not retry invalid input or other permanent errors.
- Enforce a hard bound on attempts.
- Record every attempt and the final failure.
- Keep simulated tool failures deterministic and testable.
- Ensure timeout and cancellation handling does not leave the investigation stuck indefinitely.

Do not add real network calls.

### 5. Failure recovery and termination

Support:
- Partial investigation completion when a tool is unavailable.
- Explicit failed or blocked task states.
- Overall completion, partial completion, or failure with a reason.
- A maximum task/step budget to prevent runaway execution.
- Prevention of infinite loops and repeated task execution.

Do not invent evidence to fill missing results.

### 6. Integration with existing tools

Use the existing five evidence tools through their documented contracts. Avoid rewriting them unless a demonstrated incompatibility makes it necessary. If a change is required, explain it and add regression tests.

Keep this engine generic enough for the six agents and critic feedback loop planned for Phase 3.

### 7. Tests and documentation

Add comprehensive pytest tests for:
- State transitions and validation.
- Unknown dependencies and dependency cycles.
- Concurrent independent tasks.
- Dependency ordering.
- Retryable and non-retryable failures.
- Timeouts and cancellation.
- Partial results and unavailable tools.
- Task/step limits and termination.
- Regression of the existing schemas, evidence tools, and health endpoint.

Update `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` to describe the engine and its execution lifecycle. Add or update README instructions only where needed.

## Scope limits

- No LLM agents yet.
- No real OpenAI API calls or new API key requirements.
- No frontend.
- No database, Redis, RabbitMQ, or new external services.
- Avoid a new orchestration framework unless you first explain why it is necessary and ask for approval.
- Keep the current deterministic tests and fixtures working.

## Acceptance criteria

- The engine can execute a dependency-aware investigation plan.
- Independent tasks can run concurrently.
- Retries, timeouts, failures, and termination are bounded and observable.
- Partial evidence remains distinguishable from missing or failed evidence.
- All existing and new tests pass.
- The implementation is understandable and suitable for integrating the six agents in Phase 3.

## Final report

Stop after Phase 2 and report:
A. Files created or changed.
B. Architecture and state-transition decisions.
C. A small example investigation plan and its execution results.
D. Concurrency, retry, timeout, and failure-recovery test evidence.
E. Exact commands run and full test results.
F. Known issues and unverified behaviour.
G. GO, GO WITH FIXES, or NO-GO recommendation.

Do not commit or push anything in this phase. Wait for my review.

The full suite gives **168 passed, 1 warning** (the 91 previous tests plus 77 new ones). The five tools, the schemas, the fixtures and the health endpoint are unchanged.

Before starting, I checked the repo: it was clean and in sync with `origin/main` at `8592154`, and the baseline was 91 passed with 1 warning, so there was nothing unexplained to stop for.

### A. Files created or changed
**New:**
- **Engine code:**
  - [app/orchestration/models.py](app/orchestration/models.py): task and investigation state, and the allowed transitions.
  - [app/orchestration/plan.py](app/orchestration/plan.py): plan validation (unknown dependencies, duplicates, cycles).
  - [app/orchestration/handlers.py](app/orchestration/handlers.py): the handler interface and the wrapper that runs the existing tools.
  - [app/orchestration/engine.py](app/orchestration/engine.py): scheduling, retries, timeouts and budgets.
- **Tests:**
  - [tests/test_orchestration_models.py](tests/test_orchestration_models.py): 38 tests.
  - [tests/test_orchestration_engine.py](tests/test_orchestration_engine.py): 31 tests.
  - [tests/test_orchestration_evidence.py](tests/test_orchestration_evidence.py): 8 tests.

**Changed:**
- [app/orchestration/__init__.py](app/orchestration/__init__.py): exports.
- [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md): engine design and lifecycle. Its code example is self-contained, and I ran it as written: `completed all_tasks_succeeded`.
- [README.md](README.md): one Phase 2 line and a link.

There are no new dependencies (plain `asyncio` and Pydantic, no orchestration framework). The existing evidence tools needed no changes.

### B. Architecture and state-transition decisions
- **Generic engine, pluggable handlers.** Each task has a kind, and the engine calls the handler registered for that kind. The existing tools run through one `evidence` handler; the six Phase 3 agents will become additional kinds. Nothing in the engine knows about LLMs.
- **Task lifecycle:**
  - `PENDING → RUNNING → SUCCEEDED | FAILED | CANCELLED`.
  - A task that never runs goes `PENDING → BLOCKED | SKIPPED`.
  - Any other transition raises an error. Finished states are final, so a task can never run twice, and an investigation can only be executed once.
  - Every unsuccessful finish records the reason, and the same rules are checked whenever state is built from data.
- **Dependencies.** By default a task runs only if all its dependencies succeeded; otherwise it is BLOCKED and records which dependency blocked it. An `allow_failures` policy lets a step run anyway and see what failed, which is what a final summarising step will need.
- **Retries.**
  - Only failures flagged as retryable are retried: tool `unavailable` or `timeout`, and an attempt that exceeds its time limit.
  - Invalid queries, missing data, bad input and handler bugs are never retried.
  - Each task allows at most 5 attempts, and there is also a total attempt budget across the whole investigation. Every attempt is recorded with timing, outcome, error and any evidence returned.
- **Termination and runaway prevention.** There is a deadline, a task budget and a concurrency cap. Handlers can add follow-up tasks (the hook for the Phase 3 critic loop); these are validated and counted against the task budget, which stops endless loops. When the deadline passes or the run is cancelled, running tasks become CANCELLED and unstarted ones SKIPPED, so the investigation always reaches a final state.
- **Evidence quality is separate from task success.**
  - Task status says whether the tool ran.
  - `investigation.evidence` gives one entry per evidence task: `collected`, `no_matches`, `partial`, `failed` or `not_collected`.
  - A failed task never gets an output, so evidence can't be invented to fill the gap.
  - Failed attempts keep the tool's failed result, including its `simulated` flag, as a record of what happened.
- **Final status** is `completed`, `partially_completed`, `failed` or `cancelled`, always with a reason. A task marked `required` that doesn't succeed makes the whole investigation `failed`. Tasks are optional by default, so one unavailable tool gives a partial result rather than a failure.

### C. Example plan and real execution result
The plan, for INC-2041, had five evidence tasks: `deps`, `logs`, `metrics` (payments-api and fraud-check), `changes`, and `history`, which depends on `logs`. `get_change_history` was forced to be unavailable:
```
partially_completed some_tasks_did_not_succeed attempts_used 7
  deps     succeeded  attempts=1
  logs     succeeded  attempts=1
  metrics  succeeded  attempts=1   evidence outcome: partial (fraud-check coverage gap)
  changes  failed     attempts=3   err=unavailable (simulated)   evidence outcome: failed
  history  succeeded  attempts=1   (started only after logs succeeded)
```
The event log showed the four independent tasks starting together, and `changes` retrying while the others completed.

### D. Concurrency, retry, timeout and failure-recovery evidence
- **Concurrency:**
  - Two tasks must both reach a shared `asyncio.Barrier` before either can finish. They pass; when I forced serial execution, both timed out. So the test does detect serial execution.
  - The peak number of tasks running at once equals the concurrency cap (1, 2, or 4 tasks with a cap of 5).
- **Dependency ordering:** while task `a` is held open, `b` has finished and `c` (which depends on `a`) is still `pending`. `c` then receives `a`'s output. No sleeps are used; the test holds `a` open with an `asyncio.Event`.
- **Retries:**
  - Two retryable failures followed by success gives 3 recorded attempts.
  - A failure that is always retryable stops at exactly `max_attempts`.
  - Permanent errors, crashes and bad return values are attempted once.
  - When I made the engine retry non-retryable errors, 6 tests failed, then I restored the line.
- **Timeouts:**
  - A timed-out attempt is retried and then succeeds.
  - Repeated timeouts fail only that task.
  - A 0.2s deadline cancels the stuck task, skips the task depending on it, and keeps the fast task's result.
  - Cancelling the run from outside leaves every task in a final state and re-raises the cancellation.
- **Budgets:**
  - An attempt budget of 4 is never exceeded, and the untouched task is skipped.
  - An endless "one more round" handler stops at `max_tasks=5`, with each task run once.
- **Failure isolation:** a failed task blocks its dependency chain while unrelated tasks and their outputs stay intact.
- **Flakiness:** 30 repeated runs of the orchestration tests had 0 failures.

### E. Commands and results
- Inspection: `git status --short --branch`, `git log`, `git show --stat HEAD`, and read [docs/TOOLS.md](docs/TOOLS.md) and the schemas.
- Baseline: `.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q` gave `91 passed, 1 warning`.
- Smoke run over the fixtures (C above); the documented example; the checks in D.
- **Final: `.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q` gave `168 passed, 1 warning in 1.11s`**:

  | File | Tests |
  |---|---|
  | `test_config.py` | 7 |
  | `test_fixtures.py` | 10 |
  | `test_health.py` | 4 |
  | `test_schemas.py` | 25 |
  | `test_tools.py` | 45 |
  | `test_orchestration_models.py` | 38 |
  | `test_orchestration_engine.py` | 31 |
  | `test_orchestration_evidence.py` | 8 |

  The warning is the same Starlette `httpx` deprecation notice as before.
- `/health` returned HTTP 200. `git diff --check` was clean, and a whitespace check on all new files was clean. A secret search found nothing.
- `git diff` confirms `data/`, `app/tools/`, `app/schemas/`, `app/api/`, `app/core/`, `factory.py` and `main.py` are unchanged.

### F. Known issues and unverified behaviour
- **State is in memory.** It serialises to JSON, but loading it back from JSON isn't supported yet.
- **Timed-out tool threads keep running.** When an evidence task times out or is cancelled, its worker thread finishes in the background. That's harmless for local fixtures, but Phase 3 handlers doing real I/O should use async clients.
- **Some timing paths are untested.** The optional delay between retries isn't tested (it defaults to 0). Timeout tests use real 50 ms timeouts and a 0.2 s deadline; they were stable over 30 runs, but a heavily loaded machine was not tested.
- **The concurrency cap counts tasks waiting between retries** as running.
- **Error messages keep exception text.** Handler error messages include the exception text, cut to 300 characters. Phase 3 should make sure SDK exceptions can't carry secrets.
- **Faults always fail every call.** Injected tool faults are fixed per tool, so "fails twice, then recovers" is tested only with fake handlers, not the real tools.
- **No API endpoint yet.** Investigations can't be started over HTTP.

### G. Recommendation: **GO**

[pasted text]

We are starting Phase 3 of the Orange Invest AI Engineer assignment: Six AI Agents with Genuine Collaboration.

First inspect the repository, current branch, Git status, orchestration engine, evidence tools, schemas, configuration and tests. Run the full test suite and report the baseline. Do not overwrite unexplained changes. Do not commit or push.

## Objective

Implement six distinct agents:
1. Orchestrator
2. Observability
3. Dependency
4. Reasoning
5. Critic
6. Reporting

Use the existing orchestration engine and five evidence tools. Read their real interfaces before coding; do not assume APIs that do not exist.

## 1. LLM provider integration

Use the official OpenAI Python SDK and structured outputs where appropriate, unless the project already has a compatible provider abstraction.

- Read credentials from server-side environment configuration only.
- Never expose or log API keys.
- Do not hardcode credentials, model names throughout the code, or root-cause answers.
- Keep tests deterministic without real API calls.
- Add a small provider/client abstraction so agents can be tested with fake model responses.
- If API credentials or network access are unavailable, clearly report that live integration could not be verified. Do not claim a live test passed.
- Inspect the existing dependency and configuration choices before adding or changing packages.
- Do not make real paid API calls during automated tests.

## 2. Implement the six agents with distinct responsibilities

Orchestrator:
- Builds and coordinates the investigation plan.
- Uses the existing engine and shared investigation state.
- Controls budgets, task dependencies and termination.

Observability:
- Uses logs and metrics tools.
- Returns structured observations with evidence references.
- Separates observed facts from interpretations.

Dependency:
- Uses deployment/configuration history and dependency tools.
- Identifies temporal and dependency relationships without assuming correlation proves causation.

Reasoning:
- Creates multiple plausible hypotheses when evidence permits.
- Records supporting evidence, contradicting evidence, missing information and confidence.
- References actual evidence IDs or task outputs; never invent evidence.

Critic:
- Challenges the reasoning and evidence grounding.
- Identifies unsupported claims, contradictions and alternative explanations.
- Can request targeted follow-up investigation through the existing engine's follow-up mechanism.
- Must respect task budgets, attempt budgets and deadlines.
- Must not create infinite critique loops.

Reporting:
- Produces a structured final incident report.
- Includes ranked hypotheses, supporting and contradicting evidence, confidence, facts versus assumptions, limitations and corrective actions.
- Supports an inconclusive conclusion when evidence is insufficient.

## 3. Genuine collaboration

The implementation must be more than sequential LLM calls with different prompts.

Demonstrate a real flow where:
- Evidence agents return structured findings.
- Reasoning consumes findings from both evidence agents.
- Critic evaluates the reasoning and may request targeted evidence.
- The engine executes valid follow-up tasks within existing budgets.
- Reasoning or evaluation incorporates the new evidence.
- Reporting uses the final shared state, including unresolved uncertainty.

Every agent must communicate through typed inputs/outputs and shared state or explicit task results. Preserve provenance for all evidence references.

## 4. Reliability and safety

- Validate model outputs against schemas before accepting them.
- Handle invalid structured output, provider errors, timeouts and rate-limit errors without leaking secrets.
- Retry only appropriate transient failures and respect bounded budgets.
- Do not treat missing/failed evidence as evidence of absence.
- Do not let the LLM execute arbitrary tools, shell commands or Python.
- Only allow registered evidence tools and validated follow-up tasks.
- Avoid claiming a root cause with high confidence when the evidence does not justify it.
- Review how exception messages are stored by the orchestration engine; prevent provider errors from exposing credentials or sensitive request content.

The existing tool timeout limitation involving background worker threads must not be misrepresented as solved. Since these fixtures are local, avoid adding real external I/O to tool handlers.

## 5. Testing

Add deterministic tests using fake model clients for:
- Each agent's input/output contract.
- Correct evidence references.
- Competing hypotheses and contradictory evidence.
- Critic rejection of unsupported conclusions.
- A critic-requested follow-up that changes the evidence available to reasoning.
- Critic-loop termination at configured limits.
- Insufficient evidence leading to an inconclusive report.
- Invalid model output and transient/permanent provider failures.
- Secret redaction in error handling.
- Regression of all existing tools, orchestration tests and health endpoint.

Do not depend on a live OpenAI account or real API calls for the test suite.

## 6. Documentation

Update the architecture documentation with the agent roles, data flow, feedback loop and termination controls. Document configuration, live-provider verification status and limitations.

Do not build the frontend or single-agent benchmark in this phase.

## Acceptance criteria

- Six distinct agents exist with typed contracts.
- Real collaboration and a critic-driven feedback path are implemented.
- Evidence is traceable to tool results.
- Uncertainty and contradictory evidence are represented honestly.
- Follow-up loops are bounded.
- The complete test suite passes without real API calls.
- Live LLM integration is explicitly reported as tested or unverified, based on actual execution.

Stop after Phase 3. Report files changed, architecture, an example investigation trace, exact test results, live API verification status, limitations and a GO / GO WITH FIXES / NO-GO recommendation. Do not commit or push.

### Files changed
**New:**
- **Agents** (`app/agents/`):
  - [contracts.py](app/agents/contracts.py): typed inputs and outputs of all six agents.
  - [evidence.py](app/agents/evidence.py): evidence references and their checks.
  - [base.py](app/agents/base.py): shared model-call, validation, error and fallback handling.
  - One file per agent: [orchestrator.py](app/agents/orchestrator.py), [observability.py](app/agents/observability.py), [dependency.py](app/agents/dependency.py), [reasoning.py](app/agents/reasoning.py), [critic.py](app/agents/critic.py), [reporting.py](app/agents/reporting.py).
- **Model layer** (`app/llm/`): the provider interface, the OpenAI client, a scripted fake for tests, and a settings-based factory.
- [app/core/redaction.py](app/core/redaction.py): strips credentials from stored and logged error text.
- [app/cli.py](app/cli.py): `python -m app.cli INC-2041` for a live run.
- **Tests:** [tests/test_agents.py](tests/test_agents.py) (26), [tests/test_llm.py](tests/test_llm.py) (27), and [tests/agent_scripts.py](tests/agent_scripts.py) (scripted model responses).

**Changed:**
- **Engine** (`app/orchestration/`), all additive:
  - Finalizer tasks, which start only after every other task has finished and see a copy of all tasks.
  - New model error codes.
  - Redaction of stored handler error messages.
  - An existing helper made public.
  - 5 regression tests added.
- **Configuration:** [config.py](app/core/config.py) has new LLM and limit settings, documented in [.env.example](.env.example).
- [requirements.txt](requirements.txt): adds `openai>=3.37,<4`, the only new dependency, which the assignment asks for.
- [README.md](README.md) and [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md).

`data/`, the evidence tools, the schemas, the API and the health endpoint are unchanged.

### Architecture
- **Agents are engine tasks.** Each of the six agents runs as a task on the existing engine. They share information only through typed task results in the investigation state; no agent calls another.
- **Planning.**
  - The Orchestrator's model proposes the time window, focus services and keywords.
  - Code bounds that proposal and replaces it with a default window if it's unusable.
  - Code then builds the task graph and sets every budget.
- **Evidence agents.** Observability and Dependency compute facts in code: metric summaries, minutes before the report, and dependency paths. The model then interprets them, labelling each item as fact or interpretation, with references.
- **Critic loop.** The Critic can request more evidence. Code checks each request against the real tool's query schema, rejects repeats and anything over the per-round limit, and schedules the next round of evidence, reasoning and critique. The engine's task, attempt and deadline budgets still apply on top.
- **Reporting runs last.** It is the finalizer, so it runs after every critic round, whatever happened. If it can't run, a fixed "inconclusive" report is produced from the state, so a report always exists.
- **The model never runs tools, shell commands or code.** It can only request a query, which code validates first.
- **Grounding rules enforced in code:**
  - Cited references must be ones the agent was actually shown.
  - Failed or missing evidence can't be used as support or contradiction.
  - Confidence is capped: "high" needs direct evidence from at least two sources and nothing against it.
  - The Critic's rule-based issues override a model "accept".
  - A probable cause may be named only if the final critic accepted and the top hypothesis is at least medium and clearly ahead of the rest. Otherwise the report is forced to "inconclusive".
  - Every correction code makes is recorded alongside the model's original output.
- **Provider errors.**
  - The SDK's own retries are off, so the engine is the only place retries happen.
  - Provider errors become fixed messages (error kind and HTTP status only), and the SDK exception isn't stored or chained.
  - Unexpected handler errors are redacted. A test confirms a fake key never appears anywhere in the serialised result.

### Example trace (real fixtures; scripted model responses)
```
orchestrate ─► follow-ups: ev.logs, ev.metrics, ev.changes, ev.deps.payments-api, ev.history, observability, dependency, reasoning.r1, critic.r1
5 evidence tasks run concurrently ─► observability ∥ dependency ─► reasoning.r1: H1(pool reduction)=high, H2(fraud-check+retries)=medium
critic.r1: revise ("H1 overconfident; fraud-check metrics missing") ─► requests search_logs "config reloaded" ─► follow-ups ev.c1.1, reasoning.r2, critic.r2
reasoning.r2 cites ev.c1.1#0 ("Config reloaded: db.pool.max_size=20" at 13:55:20): H1=medium, H2=low
critic.r2: accept ─► report (finalizer, last): probable_cause_identified; H1:medium, H2:low
  report adjustments: the report's attempted "high" for both hypotheses was lowered by code
completed / all_tasks_succeeded / 14 tasks / 14 attempts / 8 model calls
```

### Test results
- **Commands:** `.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q`, plus a second full run with `OPENAI_BASE_URL` pointed at a closed local port so any real API call would fail.
- **Result:** **226 passed in 2.05s, no warnings.**

|---|---|
| `test_agents.py` | 26 |
| `test_llm.py` | 27 |
| `test_orchestration_engine.py` | 36 |
| `test_orchestration_models.py` | 38 |

- **Coverage:** every item in your testing list has a test, including critic rejection of unsupported conclusions, a critic request that changes what Reasoning sees, loop termination at the round limit and at the task budget, insufficient evidence leading to an inconclusive report, and transient and permanent provider failures.
- **Guardrail checks:** I disabled the confidence cap, the conclusion rule and the critic round limit one at a time. Each caused test failures (1, 4 and 2), and I restored them afterwards.
- **Stability:** 20 repeated runs of the agent, engine and LLM tests had 0 failures. `/health` returned 200, and `git diff --check` and the whitespace checks are clean.
- **The Starlette warning is gone.** That's a side effect of `openai` pulling in `httpx2`, not a deliberate upgrade.

### Live API verification: not verified
- No real model call was made. Without settings, `python -m app.cli INC-2041` exits with code 2 and makes no request. I confirmed that.
- The output schemas pass the SDK's own strict-schema converter. That is not the same as the API accepting them.
- To verify, set `LLM_PROVIDER=openai`, `LLM_MODEL` and `LLM_API_KEY`, then run `python -m app.cli INC-2041`. That is billed.

### Limitations
- **Model behaviour is untested.** I wrote the scripted responses, so the tests prove the mechanics, not model quality or how often a real model cites valid references.
- **Unmeasured costs.** Reasoning and Critic receive all collected evidence (about 90 metric samples for INC-2041). I haven't measured token cost or latency.
- **The evidence-tool thread limitation is unchanged.** A timed-out tool's worker thread still finishes in the background. Agent requests use the SDK's async client, so cancelling an agent task does cancel its request.
- **Rule boundaries:**
  - The confidence policy is a heuristic.
  - "No known relation" only means within the two-hop dependency graph that was fetched.
  - A request naming an unregistered tool invalidates the Critic's whole output (and triggers a retry) rather than being rejected on its own.
- **Prompt injection is mitigated but not adversarially tested.** Evidence is marked as data in every prompt, the model can't execute anything, and outputs are validated.
- **One weak test assertion.** In the permanent-provider-failure test, I only assert that Reasoning finished. In practice it fails because the scripted response cites observations that no longer exist.
- **No HTTP endpoint for investigations yet.**

### Recommendation: **GO WITH FIXES**
The code meets the acceptance criteria offline. The fix is one live run, `python -m app.cli INC-2041`, with your key before Phase 4, to confirm the API accepts the structured-output schemas and to see how the real model behaves under the grounding checks. I've stopped here.

### B. Test results
- **Before:** `226 passed in 2.02s`.
- **After:** `235 passed in 2.12s`, with no warnings. I ran it with `OPENAI_BASE_URL` pointed at a closed local port, so any real API call would have failed. 20 repeated runs of the agent, LLM and engine tests had 0 failures.
- **The weak test is now two precise tests:**
  - A permanent provider failure (HTTP 401) in the Observability agent:
    - Observability ends `failed` with no output, 1 attempt, code `model_rejected`, not retryable, and exactly one model call.
    - Reasoning still succeeds without any Observability findings and cites none.
    - The investigation ends `partially_completed`, and the report lists the 401 as a limitation.
  - The same failure in Reasoning itself:
    - Reasoning ends `failed` with no result. The Critic doesn't call the model and no further round starts.
    - The report is rule-based and inconclusive, with no hypotheses.
- **The tests catch the right regressions:**
  - If Reasoning silently turns the failure into a "successful" result, the Reasoning test fails.
  - If a rejected request becomes retryable, both tests fail.
  - If the tool field goes back to a fixed list, 3 Critic tests fail.

### C. Critic request validation
Validating each request independently was feasible without a redesign. Each request now goes through these checks in order, and the first failure rejects it with a recorded reason:
1. The tool must be registered. Unknown tools are never executed.
2. The hypothesis it names must exist.
3. `query_json` must be valid JSON and pass that tool's own query schema.
4. The query must not repeat one already run, in an earlier round or earlier in the same response.
5. It must fit the per-round limit.

Valid requests from the same response still run. Task, attempt, round and deadline budgets are unchanged and still enforced.

New tests:
- **Mixed valid and invalid:** an unregistered tool, a missing `end`, and an unknown hypothesis are each rejected. The two valid requests run as `ev.c1.1` and `ev.c1.2`, the Critic needs no retry, and only registered tools were executed.
- **All invalid with "revise":** no evidence tasks are created, and Reasoning reruns using the critique only.
- **All invalid with "insufficient evidence":** the loop stops (`nothing_new_to_investigate`) and the report is inconclusive.
- **Repeats:** a duplicate within one response and a repeat in round 2 are both rejected as "the same query was already run".
- **Tool list:** the names in the output schema match the tool registry.

### D. SDK compatibility
- **Version:** `openai` 3.37.0 is installed, within the declared `>=3.37,<4.0`. Nothing was upgraded.
- **Interfaces used** (confirmed in the installed source):
  - `AsyncOpenAI(api_key, timeout, max_retries)`
  - `responses.parse(model, instructions, input, text_format, max_output_tokens)`
  - `response.output_text`
  - `response.usage.input_tokens` and `response.usage.output_tokens`
- **Bug found and fixed:** `responses.parse` validates the model's text against our output model itself (`lib/_parsing/_responses.py:160`).
  - When that fails, it raises a `pydantic.ValidationError`, which is not an `openai` exception and wasn't caught.
  - The engine would have recorded it as a permanent `handler_error`, and stored pydantic's error text, which quotes the model output.
  - It's now a retryable `model_invalid_output`, and that quoted output doesn't appear in the message.
- **Not checked:** the updated Critic output schema passes the SDK's strict converter with the tool enum intact. Whether the API itself accepts it is part of the unrun live test.

### E. Live API status: not run
Nothing was configured, so no request was made and I didn't need to ask you for authorisation. `python -m app.cli INC-2041` exits with code 2 without making a request. When you want to run it:

2. Check the configuration at no cost:
   ```zsh
   .venv/bin/python -c "from app.core.config import get_settings; from app.llm import build_llm_client; build_llm_client(get_settings()); print('configured')"
   ```
3. When you're ready, tell me and I'll run `python -m app.cli INC-2041` once. It's billed: 8 model calls on the normal path, at most 16 with retries. Alternatively, run it yourself and share the summary it prints.

### F. Model calls, latency and token usage
None, because no live call was made. The client now records outcome, latency and tokens per call, and the CLI prints them after a run.

### G. Remaining risks and recommendation
- The API's acceptance of the output schemas is unverified, including the date-time fields that allow null and the new tool enum.
- Real model behaviour under the grounding checks is unknown: how often it cites invalid references, and therefore how often agents retry or fall back.
- Token cost is unmeasured. Reasoning and Critic receive about 90 metric samples for INC-2041.
- Phase 3 is still uncommitted.

**Recommendation: GO WITH FIXES.** The offline work is complete and tested. The remaining fix is the single live smoke test, once you've configured a key and authorised the billed run. I've stopped here.

- **Outcome:** `completed / all_tasks_succeeded`: 16 tasks, 17 attempts.
- **Model calls:** 9, all HTTP 200: orchestrator, observability, dependency, reasoning ×3, critic ×2, reporting.
- **Tokens:** 55,052 in and 5,743 out. Reasoning and Critic used about 8–10k input tokens per call.
- **Time:** 104 s in total. The Reporting call alone took 47 s.
- **Retry:** Reasoning's first attempt cited `dep-3` instead of `dependency/dep-3`. The grounding check rejected it, and the retry passed.
- **Critic loop:** round 1 asked for 3 more queries. All 3 were valid and ran. Round 2 still said "revise", the 2-round limit stopped the loop, and the report is inconclusive with five low-confidence hypotheses (H1 pool exhaustion, H2 fraud-check delay, and others).
- **Guardrails:** nothing overclaimed. The report needed no corrections from code.

**Findings worth fixing (not done; your call)**
1. **Reference format.** The real model used short IDs like `dep-3`. Accepting short IDs, or tightening the prompt, would avoid paying for a retry.
2. **Timeout headroom.** The 47 s Reporting call is close to the 60 s per-request timeout. Raising `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` to 120 would leave margin.
3. **Reasoning quality.** gpt-4o-mini listed the pool-size reduction (`ev.changes#2`) as evidence *against* the pool-exhaustion hypothesis, and split one explanation into several. Code can't fully catch misreadings like that; a stronger model or tighter prompts would help.
4. **Cost.** Reasoning and Critic receive every metric sample. Sending summaries instead would cut most of the input tokens.

**Changes this turn**
- I updated the verification status in [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) and [README.md](README.md).
- The suite still passes: 235 tests, `git diff --check` clean, and `.env` is git-ignored and untracked.
- The `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` line is still missing from `.env.example`. I've left it as you have it.
- Nothing is committed or pushed.

**Recommendation: GO WITH FIXES.** Live integration is verified. I'd make fixes 1 and 2 (small, no cost to test) before the evaluation phase. Any further paid run needs your approval.

Phase 3.2 is done, offline only. Agents no longer pay for a retry when they use an unambiguous short reference, and the default model timeout is now 120 s. The suite went from **235 to 257 passed**. No model calls were made, and nothing is committed or pushed.

**One thing to change yourself:** your own `.env` still has `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=60`, which overrides the new default. Set it to 120 if you want the extra headroom. I didn't edit `.env`.

**Files changed**
- `app/agents/evidence.py`: adds the rule for turning a short reference into the full one.
- `app/agents/base.py`:
  - The shared prompt now asks for the full reference form.
  - Short references are rewritten before the checks run, and each rewrite is recorded in `adjustments`.
  - Ambiguous references get a specific rejection message.
- `app/core/config.py`: `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` defaults to 120 (was 60).
- `app/agents/orchestrator.py`: the agent task timeout is the model timeout plus 30 s (150 s by default). Building the configuration from settings now fails with a clear message if the deadline is shorter than that.
- `app/cli.py`: exits with code 2 and a clear message on that error instead of crashing.
- `.env.example`: documents `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=120`.
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`: describes both changes.
- `tests/test_agents.py`: 22 new tests.

Six-agent architecture, confidence and conclusion rules, fixtures, model provider, evidence tools and engine are unchanged.

**Test results**
- Before: 235 passed. After: 257 passed, with no warnings. The full run was done with the OpenAI address pointed at a closed local port, so any real call would have failed.
- `git diff --check` is clean. `.env` is ignored and untracked, and no secrets are in the diff.
- I broke the code on purpose four ways to check the new tests:
  - They catch ambiguous references being guessed, normalization switched off, and the deadline check removed.
  - Removing the guard against guessing record references (`#n`) wasn't caught, because that guard is redundant: such references can never match a finding ID anyway. The tests do check that `#0`, `0` and `logs#0` are never resolved.

**How references are handled now**
- Full references pass unchanged.
- A bare finding ID like `dep-3` becomes `dependency/dep-3` only when exactly one finding shown to that agent has that ID. The task's `adjustments` records it, for example "reference 'dep-1' normalised to 'dependency/dep-1'".
- These are never guessed and are still rejected (which triggers a retry):
  - unknown IDs such as `dep-99`;
  - IDs for findings that agent was never shown (Observability can't resolve `obs-2`);
  - record references like `#0`;
  - a wrong prefix such as `other/dep-3`.
- An ID matching several findings is rejected with a message listing the matches.
- References still trace back to tool records, which a test confirms.

**Timeouts**
- **Model request timeout:** 120 s per request by default, overridable via `LLM_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`. A test confirms an override of 200 reaches the SDK client.
- **Engine per-task timeout:** always the model timeout plus 30 s, so the request timeout fires first and is classed as a retryable model timeout.
- **Unchanged:** retries (2 attempts per agent task) and the 300 s deadline.
- **Deadline check:** a deadline shorter than one task's timeout is now rejected. Invalid timeout values (0, −5, 601, `abc`) are rejected too.
- This only adds headroom; it doesn't make anything faster.

**Remaining risks**
- **Worst-case time grows.** One agent task can now wait 2 × 120 s before failing, so slow responses use up the 300 s deadline sooner. A report is still always produced.
- **Prompt change unproven.** Whether the stricter prompt stops short references in a live run is untested, since no live call was made this phase.
- **Ambiguity is rare in practice.** Ambiguous short IDs are only tested at unit level, because observation and dependency IDs use different prefixes.
- **Unchanged from 3.1:**
  - Reasoning quality with gpt-4o-mini, including the misread "contradicting" evidence.
  - About 55k input tokens per run.
  - The live report ending inconclusive. I didn't loosen the confidence rules to change that.

**Recommendation: GO.** I've stopped here for your review.

## Step 1: Inspect and protect existing work

1. Inspect Git status, current branch, latest commit and all uncommitted changes.
2. Run the complete test suite and record the baseline.
3. Read the assignment requirements, existing fixture formats, agent interfaces, LLM client protocol, CLI, schemas and documentation.
4. Preserve all existing work. Do not commit or push.
5. Do not run any paid live model requests without my explicit authorization.

## Step 2: Create a labelled synthetic evaluation dataset

Provide at least 10 distinct incident cases with documented expected outcomes and root-cause labels where a root cause is justified.

Cover:
- A deployment or configuration change preceding an incident.
- A misleading deployment correlation that is not the cause.
- Conflicting logs and metrics.
- Missing or delayed metrics.
- A dependency-related failure.
- A retry or timeout problem.
- A simulated evidence-tool failure.
- Multiple plausible hypotheses.
- An incident where the evidence is insufficient and the correct outcome is inconclusive.
- A case where a seemingly strong hypothesis is contradicted by direct evidence.

Reuse the existing evidence tools and fixture infrastructure where possible. Keep expected labels separate from the investigation inputs. Do not leak gold labels into prompts or tool results.

The dataset must be deterministic and runnable offline. Document the intended root cause, acceptable alternatives if appropriate, and why inconclusive is or is not acceptable for each case.

## Step 3: Implement a single-agent baseline

Create a genuine single-agent investigation approach using the same five registered evidence tools, the same incident inputs and the same model provider abstraction.

The baseline must:
- Gather relevant evidence through registered tools.
- Produce ranked hypotheses, cited evidence, confidence, limitations and corrective actions.
- Be subject to the same evidence-access rules and comparable investigation limits.
- Never access gold labels during inference.
- Never execute arbitrary code or unregistered tools.
- Support fake/scripted model responses for deterministic tests.

Do not make the baseline artificially weak just to make the multi-agent system look better. Document any differences that prevent a perfectly equal comparison.

## Step 4: Build the evaluation harness

Evaluate both approaches on the same incident cases and report:

1. Root-cause accuracy, including how inconclusive results are scored.
2. Evidence grounding: whether cited evidence exists, was available to the agent, and actually supports the claim.
3. Tool-call count.
4. End-to-end latency.
5. Input and output token usage where available.

Define every metric precisely. Report per-case results and aggregate summaries. Include failures, invalid outputs and inconclusive outcomes; do not silently drop unsuccessful runs.

Keep tests deterministic and offline by default. Separate scripted/offline correctness tests from real-model quality evaluation. Do not present scripted-model scores as proof of real LLM reasoning quality.

If live evaluation would require paid API calls, implement an explicit opt-in command for it, but do not execute that command. Report live model evaluation as pending authorization.

## Step 5: Reproducibility and tests

Add tests for:
- All incident fixtures and gold labels being valid.
- Gold labels never being passed to either investigation approach.
- Single-agent and multi-agent output normalization.
- Metric calculations, including inconclusive and failed cases.
- Grounding checks for valid and invalid references.
- Identical incident sets and comparable tool access.
- Repeatable offline evaluation results.

Avoid arbitrary sleeps and avoid introducing new dependencies unless necessary.

## Step 6: Deliverables and documentation

Create:
- Evaluation dataset and clear dataset documentation.
- Single-agent baseline implementation.
- Runnable evaluation command.
- Per-case results and aggregate comparison report.
- A concise explanation of methodology, limitations and what the results do and do not prove.

Use machine-readable output such as JSON or CSV if useful, plus a readable Markdown report. Do not fabricate benchmark results or token costs.

Do not build the frontend in this phase. If the core deliverables are complete early, report the status and wait for review before starting optional presentation work.

## Acceptance criteria

- At least 10 labelled synthetic incidents exist.
- Both approaches use the same evidence sources and incident set.
- The baseline is genuinely single-agent and not intentionally handicapped.
- The required metrics are defined and computed correctly.
- Offline tests and the full existing suite pass.
- Live-model results are clearly distinguished from scripted/offline results.
- No paid API calls are made without authorization.

## Final report

B. Dataset cases and scenario coverage.

E. Exact test commands and results.

G. Deliverables still needed before submission.

The full suite passes: **314 tests, up from 257**. No paid API calls were made. Live evaluation is built but not run, pending your authorisation. Nothing is committed or pushed.

The offline results come from a deliberately naive rule-based stand-in for the model ("the most recent change is the cause, else the most frequent error"), shared by both approaches. They show the harness works; they don't measure LLM reasoning, and they don't say which architecture is better.

### A. Files
- **New:**
  - `app/baseline/`: the single-agent baseline.
  - `app/evaluation/`: dataset builder and loader, offline heuristic model, normalisation, metrics, runner, report writer, and the `python -m app.evaluation` command.
  - `data/eval/`: 10 case folders, `labels.jsonl` and a README.

  - `evaluation_results/offline-heuristic/`: `report.md`, `results.json`, `per_case.csv`.

- **Untouched:** fixtures, tools, schemas, engine and model client are unchanged. The agent, config and CLI diffs still showing are your uncommitted Phase 3.2 changes; Phase 4 added only new files and docs.
- **No requirements document:** the repository doesn't contain the assignment brief, so I worked from your phase prompts.

### B. Dataset
Ten cases, each in its own evidence world read by the existing five tools through `FixtureStore`. All ten required scenarios are covered:
- **Config change before an incident:** EVAL-01, a payments DB pool cut.
- **Misleading deployment:** EVAL-02, where the disk filled up before the blamed deploy.
- **Conflicting logs and metrics:** EVAL-03, where inventory's own dashboards look healthy.
- **Missing metrics:** EVAL-04, fraud-check timeouts with its metrics absent.
- **Dependency failure:** EVAL-05, the email provider returning 503.
- **Retry storm:** EVAL-06, retries without backoff overloading inventory.
- **Simulated tool failure:** EVAL-07, with the metrics tool down for both approaches.
- **Multiple plausible causes:** EVAL-08, two changes minutes apart.
- **Insufficient evidence:** EVAL-09, where inconclusive is the correct answer.
- **Strong hypothesis contradicted:** EVAL-10, where errors persist after the suspected deploy is rolled back.

Each label states the root cause, any acceptable alternatives, whether "inconclusive" is acceptable and why, the distractors, and the key evidence. A test checks that every key-evidence item exists in that case's data.

Labels are kept away from the investigations:
- They sit in `labels.jsonl`, outside every case folder.
- The runner loads them only after all investigations finish; a test enforces that order.
- Another test checks that no model request contains label text.

### C. Baseline design
One model works in a loop:
1. Each step it sees the incident, the tool catalogue, its remaining budget and all evidence gathered so far.
2. It either requests tool calls or writes the final report.
3. Each tool call is checked individually (registered tool, valid query, not a repeat). Rejections are reported back to the model at the next step.

It uses the same tools, model client, shared evidence rules, confidence cap and report format as the multi-agent system. Its budget is up to 6 steps and 14 tool calls, with the same timeouts and deadline.

**The one built-in difference:** with no critic, the multi-agent rule "naming a cause requires the critic's acceptance" has no equivalent. Its other conclusion rules are the same. `docs/EVALUATION.md` lists all asymmetries.

### D. Metrics and offline results
`docs/EVALUATION.md` defines every metric exactly:
- strict and lenient accuracy, and the rate of confidently wrong conclusions;
- failure rate (crashed runs are recorded, never dropped);
- top-1 and top-3 hypothesis hits, and how often a distractor is ranked first;
- key-evidence recall, and whether cited evidence exists and was usable, plus a rough word-overlap support check;
- tool calls, model calls, latency and tokens.

Offline (heuristic) results, identical for both approaches by construction:

| Metric | Both approaches |
|---|---|
| Strict accuracy | 60% |
| Confidently wrong | 40% (EVAL-02, 04, 05, 10: the naive rule picked the distractor deploy) |
| Top-3 hypothesis hit | 89% |
| Cited references that exist | 100% |
| Tool calls per case | 4.9 |
| Model calls per case | 5.9 multi-agent vs 2 baseline |
| Tokens | n/a (none sent; none reported) |

### E. Tests
`.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q` gives **314 passed**, also run with the OpenAI address pointed at a closed port to prove no network use. 10 repeated runs of the evaluation tests had 0 failures, and the offline results are identical across runs apart from timing. Whitespace checks are clean, `.env` is ignored and untracked, and the API key appears in no new or changed file.

I checked the new tests by breaking the code on purpose:
- They catch the baseline running unregistered tools, every inconclusive being scored as acceptable, and failed runs being dropped.
- Loading labels before the investigations at first went unnoticed, so I added a test that enforces the order.

### F. Limitations
- **No real-model results yet.** These are the comparison that matters.
- **Dataset bias.** The cases are small and synthetic, with one run per case. I wrote both systems and the cases, so they may be unconsciously biased.
- **Keyword matching** can misjudge paraphrased answers, and the support check only looks at shared words. Live results need a manual read of `results.json`.
- **The baseline's token use is unmeasured.**

### G. Still needed before submission
1. **Your go-ahead for the live evaluation**, `python -m app.evaluation live --confirm-billed`. That's 20 investigations. The only measured cost is one multi-agent run: about 55k input and 5.7k output tokens, and 104 s. A single case first (`--cases EVAL-01`) is a cheaper check.

3. Committing Phase 3.2 and Phase 4.

# Phase 5 — Product-First Hardening of the Incident Investigation System

We are prioritizing the actual product over evaluation experiments. Phase 4 is complete offline, with 314 passing tests. Do not start the live evaluation or make paid model calls.

## 1. Start with a product audit

Before changing code:

1. Inspect the current Git branch, status, recent commits and complete uncommitted diff.
2. Preserve all existing Phase 3.2 and Phase 4 work. Do not discard or overwrite it.
3. Read README.md, docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, docs/EVALUATION.md, the app factory, API routes, orchestration engine, agent implementations, tools and tests.
4. Run the existing test suite and record the baseline.
5. Identify what a real user can currently do from incident submission through the final investigation report.

Create a concise `docs/PRODUCT_AUDIT.md` identifying:
- What already works end to end.
- Missing product-critical capabilities.
- Reliability, security and usability gaps.
- The highest-priority implementation plan.

Do not stop after writing the audit. Continue implementing the highest-value product improvements.

## 2. Product objective

A user should be able to submit a production incident and receive a useful, evidence-backed investigation report containing:

- Incident summary and impact.
- Ranked root-cause hypotheses.
- Confidence and supporting evidence for each hypothesis.
- Contradicting evidence and alternative explanations.
- Explicit facts versus assumptions.
- Recommended immediate mitigation and follow-up actions.
- Missing evidence, investigation limitations and an inconclusive outcome when justified.

Every cited finding must be traceable to evidence actually retrieved during that investigation. Never invent evidence, claim certainty without support, or expose secrets.

## 3. Inspect before implementing

Check whether the current application already supports these capabilities. Reuse existing implementations where possible; do not duplicate the orchestration engine, evidence tools, agent contracts or evaluation framework.

Prioritize the following gaps in this order:

1. **End-to-end usability:** a clear, documented way to submit an incident, start an investigation and retrieve the finished report.
2. **API reliability:** request validation, appropriate HTTP status codes, clear error responses, bounded execution time and consistent handling of incomplete investigations.
3. **Investigation quality:** verify that hypotheses, citations, confidence, contradictory evidence and inconclusive outcomes are handled correctly.
4. **Failure recovery:** exercise unavailable tools, partial data, timeouts, invalid model output, exhausted retries and critic follow-ups.
5. **Security:** verify secret redaction, safe error responses, input validation, no arbitrary model-directed tool execution and no sensitive data in logs.
6. **Developer experience:** setup instructions, environment configuration, example incident, documented commands and a predictable local run.

If a synchronous investigation endpoint would block for too long, assess whether a bounded background job and job-status/result endpoints are justified. Do not introduce unnecessary infrastructure or dependencies merely for architectural sophistication.

## 4. User interface decision

First establish whether a usable interface already exists. If the product currently exposes only a CLI/API, determine the smallest useful interface for submitting an incident and reading its report.

Do not begin a large frontend rewrite. Prioritize a working end-to-end product, readable reports and reliable execution. If a minimal web interface is practical within the existing stack, implement it only after the API and investigation flow are reliable.

## 5. Testing requirements

Add focused tests for every changed behavior, including:

- Valid and invalid incident submissions.
- Successful investigations and inconclusive reports.
- Evidence citations and unsupported claims.
- Partial evidence and tool failures.
- Timeouts and bounded retries.
- Safe errors and secret redaction.
- API response consistency and report retrieval, if applicable.

Use deterministic fake/scripted model responses and fixtures. No live model calls, paid API calls or external service dependencies during tests.

Run the complete test suite and report the actual result. Do not claim a test passed unless it was run.

## 6. Scope controls

- Do not run `python -m app.evaluation live --confirm-billed`.
- Do not use real customer or production incident data.
- Do not hardcode API keys or modify my local `.env` secrets.
- Do not remove tests or weaken validation to make the suite pass.
- Do not add speculative features, microservices, databases or queues without a demonstrated product need.
- Do not commit or push changes.
- Keep the evaluation dataset and Phase 4 reports intact.
- Update README and architecture documentation only where implementation changes require it.

## 7. Final report

At the end, provide:

1. Product audit findings and what you implemented.
2. Exact files added or changed.
3. How to run the product locally, submit an incident and read its report.
4. API routes or CLI commands actually available.
5. Full test results and any unverified behavior.
6. Remaining blockers and known limitations.
7. A clear GO, GO WITH FIXES or NO-GO verdict.

Work on the product's actual reliability and usability, not merely the appearance of progress. Stop after implementation and report the results; do not begin another phase automatically.

### Assistant

You can now submit your own incident, by web page, API or CLI, and get back a readable report where every citation shows the evidence it points to. The full suite passes: **350 tests, up from 314**. No paid model calls were made and nothing was committed. The Phase 3.2 and Phase 4 work is intact, including the evaluation dataset and results.

### 1. What the audit found, and what I built
Before this phase, the only HTTP route was `/health`. The CLI only took the three built-in incident IDs, needed a real API key, and printed either a terse summary or a raw 160 KB JSON dump with bare citation IDs. The full audit is in `docs/PRODUCT_AUDIT.md`.

What I built, reusing the existing agents, engine and tools unchanged:
- **Background jobs.** An investigation takes about 100 s with a real model, so the API runs it as a background job instead of blocking. Limits:
  - at most 2 at once (more get HTTP 429, not an unbounded queue);
  - a hard time limit of the deadline plus 60 s;
  - the last 50 jobs are kept.
- **Readable report.** Every citation is resolved to the actual evidence line, for example `ev.c1.1#0 … Config reloaded: db.pool.max_size=20`. It's laid out as incident and observed impact, ranked hypotheses with supporting and contradicting evidence, facts vs assumptions, immediate, follow-up and preventive actions, missing evidence, and limitations. Any citation that doesn't resolve is flagged, never hidden.
- **Keyless mode.** `LLM_PROVIDER=offline-heuristic` runs the existing rule-based stand-in. Every report from it says it is not an AI model.
- **Input limits.** Summary up to 2,000 characters, context up to 5,000, up to 10 services. Service names must look like identifiers; that check caught a real gap, since `"Not A Service!"` was accepted at first.
- **Safe errors.** Errors return fixed or redacted messages, and logs record only exception types.
- **Minimal web page** with no external assets. All evidence and model text is inserted as plain text, never as HTML.

### 2. Files
- **New:**
  - `app/product/`: `sources.py`, `service.py`, `report_view.py`, `__init__.py`
  - `app/api/routes/investigations.py`, `app/api/routes/ui.py`
  - `app/schemas/investigation_api.py`, `app/static/index.html`
  - `examples/incident.json`, `docs/PRODUCT_AUDIT.md`
  - `tests/test_api_investigations.py`, `tests/test_report_view.py`
- **Changed:** `app/factory.py`, `app/cli.py` (rewritten on the same job service; `INC-2041` and `--json` still work), `app/core/config.py` (adds `API_MAX_ACTIVE_INVESTIGATIONS`), `.env.example`, `README.md`, `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`.

### 3. How to run it
`LLM_PROVIDER=offline-heuristic python -m uvicorn app.main:app --port 8000` starts it without a key and without editing your `.env`. Then use any of:
- the web page at http://127.0.0.1:8000/;
- `curl -X POST …/investigations -d @examples/incident.json`, then `GET …/investigations/{id}/report.md`;
- `python -m app.cli --incident-file examples/incident.json`.

With `LLM_PROVIDER=openai` in `.env`, the same commands use the real model. That's billed, about 100 s per investigation.

### 4. Routes and commands
| Route | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `POST /investigations` | Submit an incident: 202, or 422 / 429 / 503 |
| `GET /investigations` | List jobs |
| `GET /investigations/{id}` | Job status, outcome and model usage (404 if unknown) |
| `GET /investigations/{id}/report` | JSON report; `?format=markdown` for Markdown (409 until finished) |
| `GET /investigations/{id}/report.md` | Markdown report |
| `GET /evidence-sources` | The evidence worlds, with example incidents |
| `GET /capabilities` | Whether investigations can run, and with which model |
| `GET /health` | Liveness |
| `GET /` | Web page |
| `/docs` | Interactive API documentation |

CLI: `python -m app.cli --list | <example-id> | --incident-file F [--source S] [--format summary|markdown|json]`.

### 5. Tests and verification
- **Suite:** `pytest` gives **350 passed**, also with the OpenAI address pointed at a closed port, so no network was used. 10 repeated runs of the new tests had 0 failures.
- **What the new tests cover:** valid and invalid submissions; successful, inconclusive and partial-evidence reports; tool failure; invalid model output with bounded retries; the engine deadline; the hard time limit; the 429 limit; 404 and 409 responses; redaction in responses and logs; and that the evidence sources never expose labels.
- **Mutation checks:** I removed five safeguards one at a time (the concurrency limit, safe error messages, flagging of unresolved citations, the offline notice, and the required `reported_at`), and each removal made tests fail.
- **Real server:** I started it with uvicorn and drove it with `curl`: submit, poll, Markdown report, 422 and 404 all behaved correctly, and the API key doesn't appear in the server log.
- **Not verified:**
  - The web page in a real browser; its script only passed a syntax check (`node --check`).
  - The API with the real OpenAI model.

### 6. Remaining limitations
- All evidence is synthetic; there are no real monitoring integrations.
- There is no authentication.
- Jobs live in memory, so they are lost on restart and limited to one process.
- A timed-out evidence tool's thread still finishes in the background.
- Report quality with a real model is unproven: the one live run was inconclusive, and the live evaluation hasn't been run.

### 7. Verdict: **GO WITH FIXES**
The product works end to end and is tested offline. Before a demo, two checks remain: load the web page in a browser, and with your authorisation run one real-model investigation through the API.
