### Assistant

I ran exactly one billed investigation of EVAL-01 through the product API, and it completed technically cleanly. **The model ranked the correct root cause first but concluded "inconclusive"**, which EVAL-01's gold label counts as a miss. My audit also found some citations that misrepresent their evidence and two "missing evidence" claims that are false.

### Execution (measured)
| | |
|---|---|
| Incident / job | `EVAL-01` / `inv-f60feafed333`, evidence source `EVAL-01` (no simulated faults) |
| Provider / model | OpenAI / `gpt-4o-mini` (from your `.env`, unmodified) |
| Submitted / finished | 2026-10-27T03:55:49.653Z → 03:56:57.307Z UTC: **67.7 s** server-side (71.3 s as seen by the client, which polled every 5 s) |
| Final status | job `completed`; investigation `completed / all_tasks_succeeded`; 16 tasks, 17 attempts, none failed |
| Model calls | **9**, all HTTP 200: orchestrator, observability, dependency, reasoning ×2, critic ×3, reporting |
| Internal retries | **1**: the second critic review was asked twice (identical 8,739-token inputs). The provider returned 200, so our own output check rejected the first answer. The exact reason isn't exposed by the API, so it's **not measured** |
| Critic rounds | 2. Round 1 requested 3 follow-up queries (2 returned data, 1 found nothing). Round 2 said "revise" when the round limit was reached |
| Tokens | **53,390 input + 5,493 output = 58,883 total** (reported by the provider) |
| Cost | **Not available**: the API reports tokens only, and I haven't applied a price |
| Secrets | the key appears in none of the server log, job, report or request files |

Exactly one `POST /investigations` was made. The job list afterwards held only this job, and nothing else could start a second.

### What the report says (the model's ranking)
1. **Connection pool saturation due to reduced pool size: high confidence.**
2. External dependency (fraud-check) latency: medium.
3. Increased load on payments-api: medium.
4. Legacy bug: low, with no evidence cited.
5. Database performance: low.

- **Conclusion:** "inconclusive", although the report's own summary calls the pool change a "strong likelihood".
- **Immediate action:** restore the pool size to 40. That's proportionate and correct.
- **Follow-ups:** sensible monitoring.

### Scored against the gold label (read only after the run)
- **Top hypothesis matches the gold cause** (the pool cut from 40 to 8), and it cites both key evidence items: the change record CHG-E01-2 and the "Connection pool saturated: 8/8" log.
- **Scored outcome: `missed_inconclusive`.** EVAL-01 doesn't accept inconclusive. The cause is that the critic still said "revise" at its round limit, and a probable cause can only be named after critic acceptance. My judgement: inconclusive isn't justified here. The evidence is direct and consistent, and the competing hypotheses have no real support.

### Citation audit
**Measured:** 26 citations (17 tool records, 9 agent findings). All resolve to evidence retrieved in this run; none are invented or unresolved.

**My judgement of whether they support their claims:**
- **H3's main support contradicts it.** It cites a request rate of 123.42 rps as evidence of increased load, but the request rate was flat (about 117–123 rps all window).
- **H2 is medium confidence on topology alone.** Its only support is a dependency edge ("payments-api depends on fraud-check"). There are no fraud-check errors in EVAL-01. Its "contradicting" citation (a 504 from the gateway) doesn't contradict it either.
- **Misleading citation in observed impact:** it says the error rate peaked at 7.65% "and was 7.61% at 10:00, indicating an increase". That pair doesn't show an increase; the 0.2% baseline isn't cited.
- **Two false "missing evidence" limitations:** the report says request-rate metrics and postgres database metrics were unavailable. Both were retrieved: the main metrics query returned all 5 series, and the postgres CPU stayed flat at about 28–32%. That flat CPU would have counted against H5, and it wasn't cited.
- **Facts mix in interpretation:** for example "…peaked at 102%, indicating a saturated connection pool **causing timeouts**", while the assumptions section is empty.
- **A defect in my dataset:** the 102% pool utilisation is physically impossible. It comes from the fixed jitter I applied to a 100% series in EVAL-01, not from the model.

### Assessment
The product worked as built: one job, bounded, every citation traceable, nothing leaked. The quality problems are real, and they repeat the earlier live run's pattern:
- the critic keeps demanding revision, so the report ends up inconclusive;
- the model misreads evidence (a flat request rate cited as "increased load");
- confidence rules count a dependency edge as direct evidence;
- the model's claims about what evidence was missing aren't checked against what was actually retrieved.

These are measured results from **one** run of a small model; it's an example, not a benchmark.

All changes remain uncommitted, and no further paid calls were made. Full artifacts are in the scratchpad:
- <scratchpad>/live_report.md
- <scratchpad>/live_report.json
- <scratchpad>/live_job.json
- <scratchpad>/live_api_server.log

# Phase 6 — Fix Live-Run Quality Defects Without More Paid Calls

The single authorized live investigation is complete. Do not make any more paid model calls or run live evaluation.

The actual EVAL-01 result exposed these defects:
1. The correct root cause ranked first, but the final outcome was incorrectly inconclusive because the critic still requested revision at its round limit.
2. A flat request-rate metric was cited as evidence for increased load.
3. A dependency relationship was treated as support for a latency hypothesis without direct incident evidence.
4. The report falsely claimed request-rate and PostgreSQL metrics were unavailable, although they had been retrieved.
5. Interpretation was mixed into facts, and the assumptions section was empty.
6. EVAL-01 has a dataset defect: jitter makes pool utilisation reach 102%, although the source series is capped at 100%.

Preserve all existing uncommitted Phase 3.2, Phase 4, Phase 5 and browser verification work. Do not reset, clean, commit or push anything.

## Step 1 — Inspect evidence and reproduce defects offline

- Inspect Git status and the full diff.
- Read the actual saved live artifacts if they are accessible, including `live_report.md`, `live_report.json`, and `live_job.json` under the existing scratchpad directory. Do not fabricate or reconstruct missing artifacts.
- Inspect EVAL-01's fixtures, labels, evidence tool outputs, report-generation logic, critic logic, and tests.
- Run existing tests before changes and record the baseline.
- Do not call OpenAI, modify `.env`, or use production data.

## Step 2 — Fix the synthetic dataset

Correct the EVAL-01 pool-utilisation fixture or jitter logic so the generated utilisation cannot exceed 100% if that metric represents a bounded percentage. Preserve the underlying causal signal and all valid gold-label evidence. Add tests for the bound and for repeatable dataset generation. Update any affected offline evaluation results only by rerunning the offline evaluation; document changed scores rather than hiding them.

## Step 3 — Make evidence availability authoritative

A report must not say a source or metric is missing if the investigation successfully retrieved it.

- Derive available and unavailable evidence from actual tool outcomes and the evidence gathered in shared investigation state.
- Distinguish an attempted query returning no matches from a query that failed, timed out, or was never run.
- Ensure report generation receives a structured inventory of evidence sources, query outcomes, and retrieved finding references.
- Validate missing-evidence claims against that inventory. Reject or correct contradictions and explain which sources were genuinely unavailable.
- Do not treat a failed query as proof that the underlying signal is absent.

Add deterministic tests for retrieved metrics, no-match queries, failed tools, timed-out tools and unattempted sources.

## Step 4 — Improve citation and hypothesis grounding

- Require evidence for each substantive hypothesis claim and material causal statement.
- Distinguish direct evidence, contextual evidence, and contradictory evidence.
- A dependency edge proves a relationship, not that the dependency was slow or failing during this incident.
- A metric supports an increase only when the retrieved values or a valid comparison demonstrate an increase. Flat values must not be presented as increasing.
- Check that the cited observation actually supports the specific claim, not merely that the reference exists.
- Do not implement simplistic keyword overlap as the sole truth test. Use deterministic checks where possible and clearly label any semantic check that remains model-dependent.
- Ensure contradictory evidence is retained and presented rather than omitted.

Add tests for flat versus rising metrics, topology-only evidence, direct error evidence, contradictory evidence, unresolved citations and citations that resolve but do not support their stated claims.

## Step 5 — Fix critic termination and final decision logic

Investigate why the critic repeatedly requests revision and why reaching the round cap forces an inconclusive result.

Implement a bounded and explicit final-adjudication policy. At the round limit:
- Do not automatically accept the critic's last verdict.
- Do not automatically force an inconclusive result solely because the critic still says "revise".
- Evaluate whether the leading hypothesis has direct, incident-specific evidence, whether material contradictions remain, whether credible alternatives have evidence, and whether the requested follow-up evidence was actually obtained.
- Accept a cause only when the documented sufficiency conditions are met.
- Otherwise retain an inconclusive outcome and state exactly which unresolved issue prevents a conclusion.

Do not simply lower confidence thresholds, remove the critic, or force acceptance of the top-ranked hypothesis. Make the decision auditable in the final report and preserve a safe inconclusive path.

Add deterministic tests for: strong direct evidence with no material contradiction; credible competing causes; direct contradictory evidence; failed follow-up evidence; and critic revision at the round limit.

## Step 6 — Separate facts, interpretations and assumptions

Ensure the report schema and renderer distinguish:
- Observed facts supported directly by retrieved evidence.
- Interpretations or causal inferences.
- Assumptions and unverified premises.
- Evidence that would confirm or disconfirm the leading hypothesis.

Do not put causal conclusions in the factual section unless directly observed. Do not leave assumptions empty merely because the model omitted them; explicitly represent an empty list only when no material assumptions were used.

## Step 7 — Verification and report

- Use only fixtures, scripted/fake model responses and offline tests.
- Run the complete test suite and `git diff --check`.
- Rerun the offline evaluation if the dataset or evaluation behavior changed. Label those results as offline heuristic results, not real-model quality.
- Do not claim that these fixes improve real-model accuracy until separately verified with authorized live testing.
- Preserve the saved live-run artifacts unchanged; create a separate analysis or follow-up report if useful.
- Do not commit or push.

Return:
1. Root causes of each defect.
2. Exact files changed.
3. Tests added and actual full-suite result.
4. Offline evaluation changes, if any.
5. Remaining model-dependent limitations.
6. A GO / GO WITH FIXES / NO-GO verdict.

Stop after this phase. Do not start another phase automatically.

### 1. Root causes and fixes
| # | Defect | Root cause | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correct cause ranked first, but reported inconclusive | A cause could only be named if the critic's last verdict was "accept". A critic still saying "revise" at its round limit forced inconclusive regardless of evidence. | A new **adjudication** step names a cause only when every check passes: a completed review, a leader at least medium confidence and clearly ahead, direct support from at least two independent sources, no strong contradiction, no comparably supported alternative, and all requested follow-ups obtained. Critic objections that cite no direct evidence are listed as unresolved but don't block. Every check appears in the report as PASS or FAIL. |
| 2 | Flat request rate cited as evidence of "increased load" | Every tool record counted as direct support; metric values were never checked | A metric sample counts as direct only if it is at least 25% from its own baseline. A flat one is removed from support, or moved to contradicting when the hypothesis claims an increase. |
| 3 | A dependency link supporting a "fraud-check latency" hypothesis | Same rule: a dependency link counted as direct support | Dependency links and past incidents now count as context only. A hypothesis with no direct support is capped at low confidence, and the critic flags it. |
| 4 | Report claimed request-rate and Postgres metrics were unavailable | The reporting model never saw what was actually retrieved, and its limitations were never checked | An **evidence inventory** built from tool results. It distinguishes retrieved, partial, matched nothing, failed, timed out and never run. Claims that retrieved evidence was missing are removed, and each removal is recorded. |
| 5 | Interpretation mixed into facts; no assumptions | The report had nowhere to put interpretations and nothing checked facts | New report sections: interpretations, and what would confirm or rule out the leading hypothesis. Facts with causal wording ("indicating", "causing") move to interpretations. If no assumptions are given while a cause is proposed, the causal-link assumption is added and marked "(added by code)". |
| 6 | 102% pool utilisation | The dataset builder added ±2% jitter on top of 100% | Percentages are capped at 0–100 and the dataset regenerated. The 38% → 100% signal is intact and the labels are unchanged. |

One policy choice to review. My first adjudication blocked on *any* direct counter-evidence, which would penalise a model for honestly listing evidence against its own hypothesis. It now blocks only when the evidence against spans at least as many independent sources as the evidence for. Weaker counter-evidence is still kept and shown in the report.

The single-agent baseline uses the same rules, so the comparison stays fair.

### 2. Files
- **New:**
  - `app/agents/adjudication.py`, `inventory.py`, `grounding.py`, `report_rules.py`
  - `tests/test_phase6_quality.py`
  - `docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md`
- **Changed:**
  - Agents: `app/agents/evidence.py`, `reasoning.py`, `critic.py`, `reporting.py`, `base.py`, `contracts.py`.
  - Baseline and evaluation: `app/baseline/single_agent.py`, `app/evaluation/dataset_builder.py`, `heuristic_model.py`, `normalize.py`.
  - Product: `app/product/report_view.py` and `app/static/index.html`. Each citation now shows its evidence type, and the report and page show the decision and evidence-availability sections.
  - Data: `data/eval/cases/EVAL-01, 02, 05, 07, 08/metrics.jsonl`, regenerated, and `evaluation_results/offline-heuristic/*`, rerun.
  - Tests: `tests/agent_scripts.py`, `test_agents.py`, `test_baseline.py`.
  - Docs: `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`, `EVALUATION.md`, `PRODUCT_AUDIT.md`, `data/eval/README.md`.

### 3. Tests
- **New (39 tests).** An offline replay feeds a scripted model the live run's mistakes on EVAL-01's real evidence and checks each is now caught. Unit tests cover:
  - flat vs rising metrics; topology, history and missing evidence;
  - the inventory statuses, including timeouts and never-run queries;
  - removing false "missing evidence" claims while keeping true ones;
  - every adjudication scenario you listed;
  - the facts / interpretations / assumptions split;
  - the 0–100% bound.
- **Mutation checks:** I reintroduced each defect on purpose, one at a time, and each made tests fail.
- **Four older tests were updated**, not removed. Three expected wording or fields that changed. The fourth, `test_critic_loop_stops_at_max_rounds`, used to require "inconclusive" when the critic says "revise" at its limit. That was exactly defect 1, so it now asserts the evidence-based decision.
- **Results:** `pytest` gives **395 passed** with the network blocked, and 10 repeat runs had 0 failures. `git diff --check` is clean, `.env` is untracked and ignored, and the API key appears in no file.
- **Browser:** the updated page passed **33 of 33** real-browser checks, with no unexpected console errors.
- **Schemas:** the extended report schema passes the OpenAI SDK's strict-schema converter. That isn't proof the API accepts it.

### 4. Offline evaluation changes
These are rule-based stand-in results, not real-model quality. Both approaches changed identically:
- strict accuracy fell from 0.6 to 0.4;
- confidently wrong conclusions fell from 0.4 to 0.2;
- lenient accuracy is unchanged at 0.6.

The cause is the new two-source requirement. The naive stand-in often cites only a change record, so it now stays inconclusive:
- **EVAL-03:** correct → missed inconclusive;
- **EVAL-07:** correct → acceptable inconclusive;
- **EVAL-04:** wrong → acceptable inconclusive;
- **EVAL-05:** wrong → missed inconclusive.

**EVAL-02 and EVAL-10 remain confidently wrong** under the stand-in, because their distractor deploys also have error logs from the same service. All of this is documented in `docs/EVALUATION.md`.

### 5. Remaining limitations that depend on the model
- **Semantic fit.** Whether a log line or change actually supports the specific causal claim is still left to the model and critic. The checks are structural.
- **Metric threshold.** The 25% rule compares against the earliest third of the retrieved window. If that window starts after the incident began, a real change can look flat.
- **Keyword triggers.** Detecting "increase" claims and "missing evidence" claims relies on fixed phrase lists, so paraphrases can slip through.
- **Timing check not implemented.** A rule that symptoms starting *before* a change can't have been caused by it would catch EVAL-02. I haven't added it.
- **Live behaviour unverified.** Whether gpt-4o-mini fills the new report fields well, and whether the API accepts them, hasn't been tested.

### 6. Verdict: GO WITH FIXES
The defects are fixed and verified offline. The outstanding item is one authorised live re-run, to confirm the extended report schema and the new decision logic with the real model. Until then, I'm not claiming better real-model accuracy.

The live artifacts are still only in the session scratchpad, which is temporary. I can copy them into the repository if you want them kept.

### Step 2 — Implement temporal consistency checks

Inspect the timestamp fields available in incident metadata, changes/deployments, logs, metrics and findings.

Implement a deterministic temporal validation layer that:

- Detects when the earliest observed symptom precedes a proposed causal change.
- Prevents a later change from being presented as the cause of the initial symptom onset.
- Allows the report to say a later change may have worsened an already existing incident when evidence supports that interpretation.
- Handles missing, invalid, ambiguous, or incomparable timestamps safely.
- Accounts for time zones and timestamp precision; do not compare raw timestamp strings.
- Does not infer precise event ordering from incomplete or uncertain timestamps.
- Records the temporal check and its evidence in an auditable form.
- Does not automatically discard a hypothesis solely because timestamps are unavailable.

Do not assume that every change timestamp is a deployment timestamp. Use the actual semantics of the records.

Add regression tests, including:
1. Symptoms begin before a deployment.
2. Symptoms begin after a deployment.
3. A deployment may worsen an earlier incident.
4. A required timestamp is missing.
5. Time zones differ but timestamps represent the same instant.
6. Invalid or ambiguous timestamps.
7. A change has no causal relevance despite occurring before symptoms.

Use synthetic fixtures and scripted model responses only.

### Step 3 — Review evidence independence

Inspect the existing adjudication requirement for direct support from two independent sources.

Improve it only where justified by the current data model:

- Distinguish unique records from independent underlying observations.
- Avoid counting duplicate records, copied messages, or multiple views of the same event as independent corroboration.
- Treat independent telemetry sources as corroboration only when their provenance supports that interpretation.
- Keep evidence-source identity and event identity separate if both are available.
- When independence cannot be established, explicitly mark it as unknown rather than claiming independence.
- Preserve the safe inconclusive outcome when the evidence is insufficient.

Do not weaken the current adjudication policy simply to increase accuracy. Do not introduce arbitrary confidence boosts or count-based acceptance rules without evidence.

Add tests for duplicate evidence, repeated observations of one event, independent sources, and unknown independence.

### 3. Timeline checks
For each hypothesis that names a change as the cause, the first symptom is compared with each cited change. Times are compared as real UTC instants, never as text. A symptom is an ERROR log or a metric anomaly on the relevant services. Each change is classed as one of:
- **precedes onset**;
- **follows onset**: it can't explain how the incident began. It's described as possibly having *worsened* an existing incident only if a symptom metric rose at least 25% further after it;
- **ambiguous**: within 60 seconds, or within a metric's sampling interval;
- **unknown**: the timestamp is missing, invalid or has no timezone;
- **persisted after rollback**: the same service was later successfully rolled back, but symptoms continued more than 5 minutes after.

A hypothesis is blocked only if *every* cause it cites follows onset or persisted after rollback. Missing or ambiguous times never rule a hypothesis out. All seven required scenarios are tested, plus timestamp parsing, failed changes and a failed rollback.

**One correction to my own documentation.** I had written that EVAL-01's change "precedes the first symptom". The check actually reports **ambiguous**: pool utilisation shows saturation in the same 5-minute sample as the 09:55 change. I fixed the document to match.

**One defect found along the way.** The Phase 6 metric rule mistook normal noise on tiny values (an error rate alternating 0.2% / 0.3%) for a change. A real change also went undetected when it fell inside the window used to compute the baseline. Both are fixed by a median-based baseline with a noise threshold, and regression tests cover them.

**Limitations:**
- Changes whose effect is delayed aren't modelled.
- Symptoms that aren't ERROR logs or metric anomalies are missed.
- A concurrent, unrelated incident on the same service could set an early onset.
- The tolerances (60 seconds, 5 minutes, 25%) are fixed choices.

### 4. Evidence independence
Direct evidence is grouped into observations before anything is counted. These are merged into one observation:
- identical records retrieved by different queries;
- log lines sharing a trace ID;
- a log line that names a change's ID, version (including a "v" prefix) or config key. "Config reloaded: db.pool.max_size=8" is an echo of the change, not separate support for it.

Independent corroboration requires observations from at least two *different* evidence sources (logs, metrics, change history). Several observations from one source are reported as independence **unknown**, never assumed. If anything this makes the rules stricter: an echo no longer counts as a second source.

**Limitations:**
- Correlation between different metrics of the same service isn't modelled.
- Echo detection relies on exact IDs, versions or config keys appearing in the log text.

### 5. Live artifacts
- All three originals existed, and their checksums still matched the values recorded in Phase 6.
- I read them in full and scanned them for API keys, authorization headers, environment variable names, emails, phone numbers, IP addresses and local file paths. **Nothing was found**, so they were copied unchanged, with no sanitised version needed.
- Each copy is **byte-for-byte identical** to its original (checked with `cmp` and with matching SHA-256). The scratchpad originals are untouched.
- The server log was deliberately not copied. It isn't needed, and it contains local process details.
- The README and analysis document both state this is **one observed run, not a benchmark**.

### 6. Security checks
- `.env` is ignored and untracked, and wasn't modified (last changed 08:52, before this phase began).
- No `.env` or credentials exist under `artifacts/`.
- The API key's prefix appears in no new, changed or copied file.
- Server logs from this phase show 0 OpenAI requests.
- The UI still never inserts content as HTML.

### 7. Remaining blockers
- None of the Phase 6 or 7 logic has been run with the real model, including the extended report schema. I'm not claiming any improvement in real-model accuracy.
- All Phase 3.2–7 work is still uncommitted.

### 8. Verdict: **GO WITH FIXES**
The offline work is complete and verified. The outstanding item is one separately authorised live run, to confirm the extended schema and the new checks against the real model.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - FastAPI with an app factory (`create_app(settings, investigation_service=...)`), lifespan shutdown, pydantic v2 and pydantic-settings (SecretStr).
   - asyncio orchestration engine:
     - TaskSpec / TaskRecord state machine.
     - Retries only for retryable errors; per-attempt `asyncio.timeout`; deadline; `max_tasks` / `max_total_attempts` budgets.
     - Follow-up tasks; finalizer tasks with an `all_tasks` snapshot; redaction of handler errors.
   - OpenAI SDK 3.37.0: `AsyncOpenAI(max_retries=0)`, `responses.parse(model, instructions, input, text_format, max_output_tokens)`, strict structured outputs. The SDK raises `pydantic.ValidationError`, which is mapped to INVALID_OUTPUT. CallStats records tokens and latency.
   - `LLMClient` protocol; `ScriptedLLMClient` for tests; `HeuristicLLMClient` (offline rule-based stand-in: latest change, otherwise most frequent error).
   - Evidence references: `task`, `task#n`, `task/finding-id`. RefIndex resolves them, normalizes unambiguous short IDs, and classifies evidence as direct / contextual / no_change / missing / unknown.
   - Noise-aware metric classification: median of the earliest third as baseline; noise = max(1.4826·MAD, smallest step); change if |v − baseline| ≥ max(25%·|baseline|, 3·noise).
   - Grounding: flat metrics are removed from support, or moved to contradicting when an increase is claimed (INCREASE_CLAIM regex).
   - Evidence inventory: retrieved / partial / no_matches / failed / timed_out / not_run; validation of availability claims.
   - Adjudication checks: review_completed, hypotheses_available, leader_confident, leader_clearly_ahead, direct_support (independence), no_material_contradiction, no_credible_alternative, temporal_consistency, follow_ups_obtained.
   - Temporal checks:
     - UTC instants, TOLERANCE 60 s, ROLLBACK_SETTLE 5 min, EPISODE_GAP 30 min (episode-based onset).
     - Rollback linked only on positive evidence (`_reverts`).
     - Worsening threshold 25%.
   - Evidence independence: duplicates, same trace_id and log echoes of a change are merged into one observation; corroboration requires ≥2 different sources, otherwise "unknown".
   - Report rules: conclusion from adjudication; facts with causal language moved to interpretations; code-added assumptions; would_confirm / would_disconfirm; timeline interpretations.
   - Product layer: in-memory InvestigationService (max_active 2 → 429, hard limit = deadline + 60 s, 50 stored jobs), report view with resolved citations, Markdown renderer, static UI (textContent only, CSP), CLI.
   - Evaluation: 10 EVAL cases, labels in a separate file, strict and lenient accuracy, wrong-conclusion rate, top-1 / top-3, key-evidence recall, grounding rates, tool and model calls, latency, tokens.
   - Real-browser verification with Playwright from the npm cache (`playwright-core` 1.62.1, chromium 1234).

## Live investigation

**Measured:**
- **Run:** job `inv-ffb2e49e8b55`, `gpt-4o-mini`, from commit `9abc762`. It took 55.9 s on the server: 14 tasks, 14 attempts, none failed.
- **Calls and tokens:** 8 model calls, all accepted, with no retries. Tokens were 43,859 in and 3,989 out (47,848 total). I couldn't work out the cost because the repo has no pricing data.
- **Schema:** the live API accepted the new, longer report format. The report was written by the model, not the built-in fallback.
- **Outcome:** inconclusive. The model said the evidence was insufficient, so the code capped every hypothesis to low confidence. The three confidence checks failed.
- **Checks that passed:** direct support (5 observations from 2 independent sources), no material contradiction, no credible alternative, follow-ups obtained, and the timeline check (the config change at 09:55 came before the first symptom at 10:02).
- **Citations:** all resolved.
- **Against the answer key** (read only after the run): the correct cause was ranked first, both key evidence items were cited, and the result is still scored as a missed conclusion.

**Defects it exposed (written up, not fixed, since code changes weren't allowed this phase):**
- **Main defect:** the model's metrics query started at the incident onset, so every sample was from after the problem began. The connection pool at 100% therefore looked "normal" and was listed as evidence *against* the correct cause. The test data shows 37–38% before onset.
- **Weak citations:** one hypothesis cites the wrong supporting item, and another cites an unrelated deploy as support.
- **Over-eager fact checks:** two facts were wrongly moved to interpretations. One contains the word "indicate"; the other says "not rapidly increasing", and the check that looks for increases ignores the "not".
- **Report noise:** one limitation is listed twice, and one open question asks which metrics were missing although none were.

A successful API response and an accepted format did not produce a correct conclusion. This is a single observation and says nothing about whether the multi-agent design beats the single-agent baseline.

**Saved:** the run is in [artifacts/live-runs/EVAL-01-run2/](artifacts/live-runs/EVAL-01-run2/), uncommitted, with checksums that match. The first run's files are unchanged and their checksums still match.

# Audit: Orange Invest multi-agent incident investigator

I inspected the code, tests, configuration, git state and both saved real-model runs myself. I changed no files, made no API calls and didn't touch git. One thing I couldn't check: **the original assignment brief isn't in the repository**, so the checklist below is based on what the README and docs say the assignment requires.

## 1. Project in simple words

When something breaks in production (for example "card payments are failing"), an engineer has to dig through logs, metrics and recent changes to find out why. This project automates that investigation with a team of AI "agents" (separate AI steps, each with one job). Think of it like a hospital team: one person collects test reports, another checks the patient's history, a doctor proposes a diagnosis, a senior doctor challenges it, and someone writes the discharge summary. The AI never touches real systems. It can only ask five fixed "evidence tools" to read synthetic (made-up, realistic) data, and every claim in the final report must cite a record those tools returned. Code then checks the AI's work: are the citations real, does the timeline make sense, are two sources really independent? Only if every check passes does the report name a cause; otherwise it honestly says "inconclusive". There's also a simpler one-agent version for comparison, a test set of 10 labelled incidents, a web page, an API and a command-line tool.

## 2. How it works

```
 You (web page / API / CLI)
        │  incident: "card payments failing at checkout, ~10:05 UTC"
        ▼
 FastAPI  ──►  InvestigationService (in-memory job queue, max 2 at once)
        │
        ▼
 Orchestration engine (asyncio: runs tasks in parallel, retries, timeouts, budgets)
        │
  1. ORCHESTRATOR    decides the time window + services → code validates it
        │
        ├──► 5 evidence tools (no AI): logs · metrics · changes · dependencies · past incidents
        │
  2. OBSERVABILITY   reads logs + metrics → findings with citations
  3. DEPENDENCY      reads changes + service map → findings with citations
        │
  4. REASONING       proposes 3–5 ranked hypotheses ("H1: pool size was cut")
        │
  5. CRITIC          challenges them: accept / revise / insufficient_evidence
        │            may request extra evidence queries → back to step 4 (max 2 rounds)
        │
   CODE CHECKS       citations real? metric actually changed? timing possible? sources independent?
        │            → "adjudication": 9 PASS/FAIL checks
        ▼
  6. REPORTING       writes the report; code enforces facts vs interpretations, caps confidence
        ▼
 Report: "probable cause identified" only if every check passes; otherwise "inconclusive"
```

**The payments-api example (EVAL-01):**
1. The incident says checkout payments are slow and failing.
2. The Orchestrator picks a time window.
3. The tools return a config change at 09:55 that cut the payments-api database pool from 40 to 8 connections, timeout errors starting at 10:02 ("Timed out waiting for DB connection from pool"), and pool usage jumping from about 38% to 100%.
4. Reasoning proposes H1: the smaller pool caused the timeouts. The Critic asks, for example, "was load also higher?" and may request a follow-up query.
5. Code checks the timeline (did the change come before the symptoms?) and independence (do the change record and the error logs really come from different sources?), then decides.

| Component | Implemented | Tested | Assumption |
|---|---|---|---|
| Six agents | yes | yes, with a scripted fake model | real-model quality |
| Engine (retries, timeouts, termination) | yes | yes, 30 tests | none |
| Grounding, timeline, independence checks | yes | yes | thresholds are fixed choices (25%, 60 s, 3× noise) |
| Single-agent baseline | yes | yes | that it's a fair comparison (see risk 3) |
| API, UI, CLI | yes | yes, plus a real browser check in an earlier phase | none |

## 3. What is already working (verified)

- **All 462 tests pass offline,** with no network and no key.
- **Offline mode works end to end** through the API, the CLI and the UI. I ran it.
- **Citation checking works:**
  - every citation in both real runs resolved to a real record;
  - made-up references are rejected and retried;
  - this is tested in `tests/test_phase6_quality.py`.
- **Honest "inconclusive" results:**
  - EVAL-02 (the deploy came *after* the symptoms) is capped to low and reported as inconclusive, not wrongly blamed;
  - EVAL-07 (the metrics tool is down) is reported as a gap, not as "nothing happened".
- **The live API accepts the strict report format,** shown by run 2: 8 calls, all accepted, no retries.
- **Usage is recorded honestly:**
  - real runs record calls and tokens;
  - offline mode shows `tokens in=None` and a NOTICE that it's "not an AI model".
- **The evaluation harness runs:** 10 cases, with answer labels loaded only after the runs finish.

## 4. What is broken or risky (most severe first)

**HIGH 1: abnormal metrics can be classified as "normal".** I reproduced this with the real code, offline.
- **How it happens:** the "normal level" (baseline) of a metric is the median of the *earliest third of whatever samples were fetched* (`_classify_metric`, [evidence.py:230-247](app/agents/evidence.py#L230-L247)). The fetch window is whatever the model proposed ([orchestrator.py:183-187](app/agents/orchestrator.py#L183-L187)).
- **What I saw:** on EVAL-01 data, a window starting at 10:00 gives pool usage `100 vs baseline 100: within the normal range` and latency of 2,436 ms "+2%, normal". A window starting at 09:30 gives `100 vs baseline 38 (+163%): changed` and latency +1253%.
- **Why it matters:** in real run 2, this turned the strongest evidence for the correct cause into evidence *against* it. It also changed the timeline check: the 09:55 pool sample was never fetched, so the result says "precedes" instead of "ambiguous".
- **Tests:** none cover this case.
- **Fix:** always fetch metrics from a fixed lookback before onset. If no pre-onset samples exist, label the metric "no baseline" (context), not `no_change`.
- **Regression test:** EVAL-01 metrics fetched from 10:00 must not be `no_change` or contradicting; fetched from 09:30 they must be `direct`.

**HIGH 2 (operational): accidental paid calls.**
- **What I found:** your `.env` is set to `LLM_PROVIDER=openai` / `gpt-4o-mini` with a key present. I printed only those two names, not the key.
- **Why it matters:** a plain `uvicorn app.main:app` or `python -m app.cli …` without the `LLM_PROVIDER=offline-heuristic` prefix makes billed calls with **no confirmation**. Only `python -m app.evaluation live` asks for `--confirm-billed` ([__main__.py:55](app/evaluation/__main__.py#L55)).
- **Fix:** require an explicit opt-in such as `ALLOW_BILLED_CALLS=1` for the server and CLI.
- **Test:** with provider `openai` and no opt-in, submitting an investigation returns 503 and the CLI exits non-zero, with no client built.

**MEDIUM 3: the multi-agent vs single-agent comparison tells us nothing yet.**
- In `evaluation_results/offline-heuristic/per_case.csv`, both approaches score the same on every one of the 10 cases, because the same rule-based stand-in drives both.
- No real-model evaluation has been run.
- **Fix:** it can't be fixed without paid calls. Until then, say so plainly, as the docs already do.

**MEDIUM 4: one AI flag can override all the code checks.**
- In run 2, the direct-support, contradiction and timeline checks passed. The conclusion was still blocked because Reasoning set `insufficient_evidence=true` (the `hypotheses_available` check).
- That judgement was probably influenced by risk 1.
- This is a design choice, not a bug. Revisit it only after fixing risk 1.

**MEDIUM 5: the report's fact checker has false positives** ([report_rules.py:34](app/agents/report_rules.py#L34), [grounding.py:19](app/agents/grounding.py#L19)). I verified both:
- "Logs indicate timeouts at 10:02" is treated as causal language because of the word "indicate".
- "was not rapidly increasing" and "Latency did not rise" are treated as claims of an increase, because negation is ignored.
- Run 2 had 3 facts moved to interpretations, 2 of them wrongly.
- **Fix:** drop "indicat\w*" from the causal list, and skip increase matches preceded by "not / no / did not".
- **Tests:** a table of sentences with the expected result for each.

**MEDIUM 6: the Critic never accepted in either real run.**
- Both runs hit the 2-round limit with the verdict "revise".
- The limit works as designed, so the system terminates, but the critic loop has never converged with a real model.

**LOW 7: report polish.**
- Run 2 has two near-duplicate limitation lines that contradict each other: "adjudicated on the evidence" vs "not resolved due to insufficient evidence".
- One open question asks which metrics were missing, although none were.
- Two weak citations: H3 cites an interpretation about the config change, and H5 cites an unrelated deploy as support. The critic did flag H3 as context-only.

**LOW 8: limits for an assignment, all documented.**
- There is no authentication, so keep the server on localhost.
- Jobs live in memory and are lost on restart.
- Evidence is synthetic only.

## 5. What the two real AI runs tell us

| | Run 1 (`EVAL-01/`) | Run 2 (`EVAL-01-run2/`) |
|---|---|---|
| Code version | before the code checks (Phase 6–9) existed | after them (commit `9abc762`) |
| Calls / tokens / time | 9 calls, 53,390 in / 5,493 out, 67.7 s | 8 calls, 43,859 in / 3,989 out, 55.9 s |
| Top hypothesis | Pool saturation due to the reduced pool size, **high** | Insufficient database connection pool size, **low** |
| Other hypotheses | "Increased load", **medium**, with 3 supports (wrong: load was flat) | all low; H2–H4 have no real support, and the critic said so |
| Conclusion | inconclusive, though H1 was "high" (inconsistent; no adjudication then) | inconclusive, with 9 checks: 3 FAIL, 6 PASS |
| Metric reading | n/a (old logic) | 100% pool usage read as "normal", and counted *against* H1 (risk 1) |
| Critic | 2 rounds, ended "revise" | 2 rounds, ended "revise" |
| Citations | all resolved | all resolved; 2 weak ones |
| JSON vs Markdown vs job | consistent | consistent, apart from the duplicate limitation lines |

**What the runs prove:**
- The real integration works.
- The real model follows the strict format.
- Every citation pointed to real evidence.
- Both runs ranked the right cause first.

**What they don't prove:**
- That the system reaches correct conclusions. Both runs said inconclusive where a cause was expected.
- That multi-agent beats single-agent.
- Anything statistical: this is 2 runs of 1 case.

The pool cut is a **plausible** root cause, strongly suggested by the timing and the error text. It's correct against the answer label. But the system has never **proven** it in a real run.

## 9. One immediate next action

## Deeper evidence

- **Metric bug:** `RefIndex._classify_metric` ([evidence.py:230](app/agents/evidence.py#L230)) builds the baseline from `points[: len//3]`. The window comes from `ScopeProposal.window_start` ([orchestrator.py:183](app/agents/orchestrator.py#L183)), which is validated only for length and closeness to the report time ([orchestrator.py:261-268](app/agents/orchestrator.py#L261-L268)), not for whether it includes time before onset. The same text appears in the saved artifact at `live_report.md:44`.
- **Provider choice:** in `build_investigation_service` ([factory.py:15-35](app/factory.py#L15-L35)), `offline-heuristic` means `HeuristicLLMClient` and anything else goes to `build_llm_client`. The OpenAI client uses `max_retries=0` ([openai_client.py:48](app/llm/openai_client.py#L48)), so only the engine retries and the call counts stay accurate.
- **How to tell a real model was called:** the job's `model` field, `process.report_author == "model gpt-4o-mini"`, and non-null token counts. Offline runs show `model offline-heuristic`, `tokens in=None` and a NOTICE line.
- **Adjudication logic:** `app/agents/adjudication.py`. The timeline logic is `app/agents/temporal.py` (tested in `tests/test_phase7_temporal_independence.py`, 27 tests). The independence logic is `app/agents/independence.py`.

## Your two-minute interview answer

"I built a multi-agent system that investigates production incidents. You give it an incident, and six specialised AI agents work on a bounded async engine. An Orchestrator plans the investigation. Observability and Dependency agents gather logs, metrics, recent changes and the service map through five deterministic tools; the AI can request data but never runs anything itself. A Reasoning agent proposes ranked hypotheses, and a Critic challenges them and can request more evidence, for up to two rounds.

"My main design principle was: don't trust the model, verify it in code. Every citation must point to a real record. Code checks that metrics actually changed, that a change happened before the symptoms, and that 'two sources' aren't really the same event logged twice. The report names a root cause only if every check passes. Otherwise it says 'inconclusive' and explains why, because a confidently wrong answer during an outage is worse than an honest 'I don't know'.

"I evaluated it on 10 labelled synthetic incidents against a single-agent baseline. The offline numbers test the harness, not AI quality, and I say that explicitly. In two real gpt-4o-mini runs, the system ranked the correct cause first both times, but stayed inconclusive. Studying the second run, I found that a metric baseline taken only from post-incident data made 100% pool usage look normal. That's the next thing I'd fix. The biggest lesson was that honest evaluation matters more than a good-looking demo."

PHASE 1 — Prevent accidental billed calls

PHASE 2 — Fix the metrics baseline bug

- Preserve existing metric-classification behavior where appropriate.
- Add a deterministic EVAL-01 regression test that proves 100% pool utilization is not misclassified as normal when pre-incident data shows approximately 38%.

- Do not make a paid call in this phase.

- Ensure words such as "indicate" do not automatically turn a factual statement into a causal interpretation.
- Handle negated statements such as "was not rapidly increasing" correctly.
- Remove duplicate or contradictory limitation lines.
- Add focused regression tests and run the complete offline test suite.

## Phase 1: what changed

- **Server startup.** It used to create an SDK client at startup just to validate the settings. Now it only validates. Without the opt-in, `POST /investigations` returns 503 naming the setting. File: [app/factory.py](app/factory.py).
- **Live evaluation.** `--confirm-billed` still counts as the opt-in, so that command behaves as before. File: [app/evaluation/__main__.py](app/evaluation/__main__.py).
- **Docs for the new setting.** It's documented in [.env.example](.env.example) (as `false`) and in the docstring of [app/cli.py](app/cli.py).
- **Tests.** The new [tests/test_billing_guard.py](tests/test_billing_guard.py) has 9 tests: without the opt-in, no client is built and nothing touches the network, through the factory, the server and the CLI; the key never appears in output; offline mode needs no key; and `--confirm-billed` still works. ### Assistant

Phase 2 is done and verified offline. A metric is now compared only with readings from before the incident started. The run 2 situation, where the metrics window started at onset, no longer turns 100% pool usage into "normal". I made no paid calls and haven't committed anything.

## The fix

**The cause.** The baseline was the earliest third of whatever window the model picked. If the window started at onset, the "normal level" was the incident itself, so 100% looked normal.

**1. Fetch the earlier data.** The metrics query now starts at least 60 minutes before the reported time. In run 2's case that's 09:12 instead of 10:00. Other tools keep the model's window. The single-agent baseline gets the same widening, so the comparison stays fair.

**2. Baseline only from before onset.** Code estimates the onset without using metrics, which would be circular:
- the first ERROR-log episode (a stray isolated error doesn't count, the same rule as the timeline checks), capped at the reported time;
- if no such episode exists, the reported time itself.

The baseline is the median of the samples before that estimate. For EVAL-01 the onset is the 10:02 error log, and the pool baseline is 38.38%.

**3. An honest gap.** With fewer than 3 samples before onset, the metric is labelled "insufficient baseline". That's context, not "no change", so it can never be counted as contradicting evidence.

**4. The same baseline for the model.** The metric summary that the Observability agent shows the model used the same earliest-third rule. That's how run 2's model was told the pool baseline was 100. It now uses the same shared helper as the classifier: either "38.38, median of 13 samples before onset", or none with "insufficient baseline".

| Situation | Before the fix | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Pool at 100%, data from 09:00 | changed | changed (100 vs baseline 38.4, +161%) |
| Pool at 100%, window from 10:00 (run 2) | **no change**, "100 vs baseline 100" | **insufficient baseline** |
| The same, through the planner (fetch widened to 09:12) | no change, contradicting | **changed** |
| Request rate 123 rps | normal | normal (+3%) |

For an end-to-end check, I replayed run 2's window offline with the stand-in. On the committed code, the latency citation was *removed* from the correct hypothesis as "normal". With the fix, it's kept as a direct change.

## Changed files (not committed)

| File | Change |
|---|---|
| [app/agents/evidence.py](app/agents/evidence.py) | Onset estimate, pre-onset baseline, "insufficient baseline", a shared `metric_baseline()` helper, the widening helper. A sample fetched twice now counts once. |
| [app/agents/orchestrator.py](app/agents/orchestrator.py) | Widens the metrics query; records the reported time on the evidence tasks it plans. |
| [app/baseline/single_agent.py](app/baseline/single_agent.py) | The same widening and reported time for the single-agent baseline. |
| [app/agents/observability.py](app/agents/observability.py), [app/agents/contracts.py](app/agents/contracts.py) | The model-facing summary uses the shared baseline. `baseline` can now be empty, and a new `baseline_basis` field says why. |
| [app/agents/temporal.py](app/agents/temporal.py) | Now imports the shared constants and time parser that moved to `evidence.py`. No behaviour change. |
| [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md), [docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md](docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md) | Describe the new rule; the run 2 defect is marked "fixed offline only, not re-checked with a real model". |
| [tests/test_metric_baseline.py](tests/test_metric_baseline.py) (new) | 21 tests. |

The 21 tests cover all four scenarios you asked for:
- EVAL-01's 100% pool usage against the 38% baseline;
- a window that starts at onset;
- missing pre-onset samples (including the 3-sample boundary);
- a stable request rate.

They also cover the onset rules, the widening in both approaches, the model-facing summary, and the run 2 replay.

## Test results

- **Focused:** 21 passed.
- **Full suite:** 492 passed (471 before, plus 21 new).
- **Regression check:** in scratch copies, restoring the old baseline failed 6 tests and removing the widening failed 3.
- **Offline evaluation:** unchanged on all 20 rows (strict 0.4, lenient 0.6, wrong 0.0 for both). That's expected: the offline stand-in always starts its window 90 minutes before the report, so the old rule happened to work there. This bug only appeared when a window started at onset, as the real model did.
- **Other checks:** both checksum sets OK, `git diff --check` clean, `.env` untouched.

## Remaining risks

- **The onset is an estimate.** If an incident is reported long after it began and no error logs date it, some incident samples can still enter the baseline. The median tolerates a minority of them.
- **Earlier unrelated errors.** An unrelated error episode just before the real one can make the onset estimate too early. The worst case is "insufficient baseline", never a false "normal".
- **Slightly more model input.** When the model's window starts late, the metrics fetch is up to 60 minutes longer. For EVAL-01 that's about 9 more samples per series, for the 5 series in that case.
- **Real-model effect unknown.** Whether gpt-4o-mini now reaches a better conclusion can only be seen in the paid Phase 4 run.

**Repository state:** branch `main`, 8 files modified plus 1 new test file, nothing staged.

**Next decision:** review the diff, and tell me whether to commit it locally or change anything. After that comes Phase 3 (report-quality false positives).

## Your six questions

1. **Onset calculation.**
   - Timezones are handled correctly: everything is normalised to UTC, and the same moment written as `+05:30` and as `Z` deduplicates correctly.
   - Missing or timezone-less timestamps can't be ordered, so they're excluded, the same policy as `temporal.py`. The tools always return timezone-aware times, and the reported time is typed as timezone-aware.
   - Isolated errors before an episode are ignored. Unrelated earlier episodes and lone stray errors are the problem: findings 1 and 2.
2. **Strictly before onset:** yes, `t < onset` at [evidence.py:300](app/agents/evidence.py#L300). The 3-sample rule is applied consistently whenever the onset is known; it's not applied in the onset-unknown fallback (finding 3).
3. **Deduplication:** correct in the classifier, but not in the summary's other fields (finding 4). The window bounds are finding 7.
4. **Same calculation:** yes. Both call `RefIndex.metric_baseline()`, and the summary rounds the same median to 3 decimals. The evidence each one sees can differ (finding 5).
5. **Fairness:** the single-agent baseline's metric queries get the same widening. The only asymmetry is follow-up queries (finding 6).
6. **Timeline coverage:**
   - The moved parser and constants are imported back into `temporal.py`, and its 45 tests pass.
   - I compared the committed code with the working tree on all 10 cases in both approaches. Timelines, adjudication checks, citation classes and conclusions are identical: EVAL-01 is still "ordering ambiguous", EVAL-02 still "change follows onset".
   - **The gap:** those timeline tests build evidence without a reported time. So 32 of their baseline calculations use the old fallback, and none combines a reported time with a metric-based onset. The reported-time path is covered only by `test_agents.py` (8 calculations) and the new test file.

## Why I left out the fallback

With live run 2's window (10:00 onwards, reported at 10:12), the error-log onset at 10:02 correctly leaves only 1 sample. The fallback would then take the 3 samples before 10:12: 10:00, 10:05 and 10:10, all incident readings. I added it to a scratch copy of the fixed code to check. It produced "100 vs baseline 100: within the normal range" and failed 3 regression tests, including the one that keeps that reading out of the contradicting evidence.

Instead, I fixed the two causes directly:
- **Unrelated services:** only the affected services' errors count toward the onset.
- **Stray errors:** a single isolated error isn't treated as the start of an episode, so the reported time is used instead.

If you still want the fallback, it can only be made safe with a further condition, and I'd want your decision on that first.

## What each fix does

| Finding | Fix | Regression tests (all failed before the fix) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Unrelated service errors | The onset uses ERROR logs from the incident's affected services, or from all services if none are listed. The affected services travel on the evidence tasks the same way as the reported time. They're an extra input key the evidence handler ignores, and they're not shown to the model. | An unrelated service's errors at 09:20–09:25 leave the onset at 10:02, and the pool is "changed vs 38". Unknown affected services still count every service. The orchestrator and the single-agent baseline both pass the services along. |
| 2. Isolated errors | A lone error is not an episode, so the onset falls back to the reported time. | One stray error 40 minutes early: the onset is the reported time and the pool stays "changed". |
| 3. Unknown onset | The 3-sample minimum also applies when the onset is unknown. I removed the classifier's separate "too few samples" check, so the classifier and the model's summary give the same "insufficient baseline" message. | 0, 1, 2 and 3 eligible samples, checking both the classifier and the summary. |
| 4. Duplicate records | The summary deduplicates by normalised timestamp, keeping the first copy of each sample, the same as the classifier. | A series loaded twice gives the same count (22), minimum, peak, last value, baseline and classification as loading it once. |

- [app/agents/observability.py](app/agents/observability.py): deduplicates the summary.

## What changed

**1. "Indicate"** ([app/agents/report_rules.py](app/agents/report_rules.py))
- "Indicate" no longer counts as causal when it reports what an evidence item shows. That means an evidence noun (logs, errors, messages, records, metrics…) comes within the 5 words before it, in the same clause.
- "Error messages … indicate timeouts" stays a fact.
- These still move to interpretations: "Latency spikes indicate pool exhaustion", "This indicates a capacity problem", and "Errors appeared; this indicates exhaustion" (the evidence noun is in a different clause).
- All other causal words are unchanged ("caused", "due to", "suggests", "likely"…). So "The logs indicate the change **caused** the outage" is still moved.

**2. Negation** ([app/agents/grounding.py](app/agents/grounding.py))
- A new function, `asserts_increase`: an increase word is not a claim if "not", "no", "never", "without", "neither", "nor" or a "-n't" word comes within the 4 words before it, in the same clause.
- "Not only" and "no doubt" don't count as negations.
- A sentence counts as a claim if *any* increase in it isn't negated. For example, "Latency did not rise, but request rate increased" is still a claim.
- Both places that use the check get the new behaviour: facts in the report rules, and hypothesis grounding.

**3. Duplicate limitation** (`report_rules.py`, plus a shared constant in [app/agents/reporting.py](app/agents/reporting.py))
- When code has already written "Critic round limit reached…", a model limitation that mentions both "critic" and "round limit" is dropped, and an audit note is recorded.
- If code says nothing about the round limit, the model's line is kept. Other critic limitations are untouched.

- [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md): the known-limits entry now describes the new rules accurately.
- [docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md](docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md): run 2's defects are marked fixed offline. One correction: three facts were wrongly moved, not two.

## A. The exact command

Run once, from the project root:

```zsh
zsh <scratchpad>/run3.sh paid
```

**The only lines that enable billing:**

```zsh
LLM_PROVIDER=openai LLM_MODEL=gpt-4o-mini LLM_ALLOW_BILLED_CALLS=true \
  python -m uvicorn app.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8771 --log-level warning > "$RAW/server.log" 2>&1 &
```

- **Scope of the opt-in:** it applies to that one server process only. Nothing is exported, `.env` isn't touched, and the model is pinned to `gpt-4o-mini` for that process.
- **Same path as runs 1 and 2:** the product API, so the results are directly comparable.

**What the script does, in order:**
1. **Refuses to start** if `artifacts/live-runs/EVAL-01-run3` or the scratch output folder already exists, or if something already listens on port 8771.
2. **Starts the server** on 127.0.0.1 only, then waits for `/health`.
3. **Aborts before submitting** unless `/capabilities` reports investigations available and model `gpt-4o-mini`. Tested: an offline server made it abort with nothing submitted.
4. **Submits exactly one** `POST /investigations` with `data/eval/cases/EVAL-01/incident.json` and evidence source `EVAL-01`. curl doesn't retry, and nothing else in the script submits.
5. **Polls read-only** (GET) for at most 420 s: the 300 s deadline, plus the 60 s hard-limit margin, plus 60 s of slack. On a timeout or failure it saves what exists and **never resubmits**.
6. **Saves** the job, report JSON and report Markdown, in the same three formats as runs 1 and 2, to the scratchpad only.
7. **Stops the server and confirms it's gone.** When the script exits, it waits for the server process to end, force-stops it after 10 s, and checks the port is closed.

**The shutdown fix.** In the first offline rehearsal, my port check found the server still listening right after the script returned. It had exited moments later, so it was most likely a shutdown race, not a leak. But a billed-enabled server must never outlive the run, so I made the stop explicit and verified. Rehearsed again: "server stopped; port closed", with no server processes left.

**Rehearsal:** the full flow ran offline (`dry` mode: offline stand-in, opt-in explicitly false), with one job, completed, and the repo unchanged.

## B. Cost controls and estimate

| Limit | Value |
|---|---|
| Per-request timeout | 120 s; each agent attempt is also capped at 150 s by the engine |
| Retries | OpenAI SDK retries off. At most 2 attempts per agent task. The whole investigation is never repeated |
| Agent tasks | 8 with `AGENT_MAX_CRITIC_ROUNDS=2`, so **16 model calls at most**. Run 1 made 9 calls, run 2 made 8 |
| Output tokens | at most 4,000 per call (`LLM_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS`) |
| Input tokens | **no configured cap**. Only the model's 128k context window limits it |
| Time | 300 s investigation deadline; hard stop at 360 s |

Pricing is from OpenAI's model page: $0.15 per million input tokens and $0.60 per million output tokens.

| Scenario | Calculation | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| Run 1, measured | 53,390 × $0.15/M + 5,493 × $0.60/M = $0.0080 + $0.0033 | **$0.0113** |
| Run 2, measured | 43,859 × $0.15/M + 3,989 × $0.60/M = $0.0066 + $0.0024 | **$0.0090** |
| Expected run 3 | Like run 2, plus up to about 15k more input from the wider metrics window (about 50 extra samples, roughly 2.5k tokens, in up to 6 calls) | **about $0.01–0.02** |
| Conservative estimate (an assumption, not a limit) | Every task retried (16 calls), each with twice run 1's average input (about 12k): 190k × $0.15/M + 64k × $0.60/M | **about $0.07** |
| Hard ceiling from the configured limits | 16 calls × the 128k context window × $0.15/M + 16 × 4,000 × $0.60/M = $0.307 + $0.038 | **about $0.35** |

The ceiling is reliable because the call count is capped in code and no call can exceed the context window. The input cap itself comes from the model, not from our settings.

**Two caveats:**
- **Deprecation:** OpenAI's page lists gpt-4o-mini as deprecated. Run 2 worked today, but if the API rejects the model, the job fails after about one call, for roughly $0, and I won't retry.
- **Partial billing:** a call that times out may still be billed. The ceiling already counts it as one of the 16.

- calls, tokens, latency and cost.

The answer key (`data/eval/labels.jsonl`) is read only **after** the run, to score the top-1/top-3 ranking, key-evidence recall and outcome.

**What it can and can't show:**

- **Can't show:** reasoning quality, a benchmark, or anything about multi-agent vs single-agent. It's 1 more run of 1 synthetic case.

## 1. Call-count enforcement: 16 requests at most, confirmed

**From the code:**
- **One model call per attempt.** The multi-agent path has a single call site, [base.py:136](app/agents/base.py#L136): one `generate` per task attempt, with no internal loop. A failed check raises a retryable error and goes back to the engine.
- **One HTTP request per call.** [openai_client.py](app/llm/openai_client.py) makes one `responses.parse` per call, and the SDK's own retries are off.
- **At most 2 attempts per task.** The engine's attempt loop runs to `max_attempts` (2) for every agent task, including `orchestrate` and the `report` step, and also stops at the 80-attempt budget.
- **No third round.** The critic creates the next reasoning and critic tasks only while rounds remain. At round 2, nothing remains, so it stops at "max rounds reached".
- **So:** 8 agent tasks × 2 attempts = 16. Critic follow-up queries are evidence tools, not model calls.

**Tested with a hostile offline model** (every task's first attempt fails, every retry succeeds, the critic always asks for another round):
- exactly **16 requests**: orchestrator 2, observability 2, dependency 2, reasoning 4, critic 4, reporting 2;
- if every call fails, it's 8, because later tasks then skip the model.

**Hardening:** the 16 depends on `AGENT_MAX_CRITIC_ROUNDS=2`. With 5 rounds the same test makes 28 requests. So the paid command now pins that setting and every other cost-relevant one, overriding `.env`.

## 2. Token ceiling: not enforced by our code, so I revised it

- **Every request is standalone:** no conversation history or `previous_response_id`. A request is billed as the instructions, plus the input JSON, plus the output schema, which OpenAI also bills as input.
- **Output:** `max_output_tokens=4000` is sent with every request and enforced by OpenAI.
- **Input:** our code has **no cap**. Only OpenAI's 128k context window limits it.
- **Measured:** the largest request in the hostile offline run was 33,815 bytes, which is at most 33.8k tokens (each token is at least 1 byte).
- **Strict bound from the settings:** suppose every query returns EVAL-01's entire dataset, the critic adds 3 follow-ups of the largest tool, and every earlier output is a full 4,000 tokens (with a 1.5× margin). That gives ≤ **143k tokens per request**, which is more than 128k. So our limits alone don't keep a request under the context window, and the window is enforced only by OpenAI.

**Revised ceiling:** 16 × 143,163 × $0.15/M + 16 × 4,000 × $0.60/M = $0.344 + $0.038 = **$0.38, rounded up to $0.40**. The context-window figure ($0.35) is lower, so $0.40 covers both.

- **Expected cost:** still about **$0.01–0.02** (runs 1 and 2 cost $0.011 and $0.009).
- **Conservative estimate:** about $0.07, unchanged.
- **Caveat:** gpt-4o-mini is listed as deprecated on OpenAI's model page. If the API rejects it, the job fails early at about $0 and I won't retry.

## 3. Script integrity

The full script is shown above.
- **Exactly one POST,** at line 70. curl doesn't retry. Every other request goes to 127.0.0.1 and is a GET: the health check, capabilities, polling, and the three saves.
- **No resubmission on any path.** If the submission is rejected, the script exits (tested with a 422). A failed poll is retried as a poll and never stops the server mid-run. On a polling timeout, the script saves what exists and exits.
- **Guards run before the POST** (lines 59–66). The server must report investigations available, model `gpt-4o-mini`, and `max_active_investigations == 1`, which proves the pinned limits reached it. Tested: a model mismatch aborted with no request written.
- **The server is always stopped.** The exit handler stops it, force-stops it after 10 s if needed, and checks the port is closed. Ctrl-C, TERM and HUP also trigger it. Tested on all 5 paths (success, mismatch, rejected submission, timeout, Ctrl-C): server stopped, port closed, no processes left.
- **Since the last plan:**
  - the cost settings are now pinned on the command line;
  - the server is launched through `env`, so the recorded process ID is the server itself;
  - a poll error is now tolerated;
  - the signal handling was added;
  - the extra `max_active_investigations == 1` guard was added.

## The input-token budget

Every request is checked in [app/llm/openai_client.py](app/llm/openai_client.py) **before** it's sent. The check covers the instructions, the evidence and earlier agent outputs (they're inside the input JSON), and the output schema. **Default:** `LLM_MAX_INPUT_TOKENS=64000`, configurable between 1,000 and 128,000.

**How the bound is computed:**
- **Instructions and input:** their size in UTF-8 bytes. This part is exact: gpt-4o-mini's tokenizer is byte-level, so a token covers at least one byte and these parts can never be more tokens than bytes. No tokenizer library is needed.
- **Output schema:** counted twice, as a margin for how OpenAI renders it.
- **Message framing:** a fixed 1,000-token allowance.

**What happens when a request is over the budget:**
- It's refused unsent, as a non-retryable `rejected` error, so it isn't retried.
- The agent falls back, and the warning is logged.

**Logging and docs:** each sent call logs its bound next to the input tokens OpenAI reports, so the margins can be checked after the run. The setting is documented in `.env.example` and in [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md).

**Sizing:** the largest bound in realistic offline runs is 36,302, from the critic in the worst case. A replay of live run 1's outputs peaks at 35,290. So 64,000 leaves about 1.8× headroom. The real token counts are about a quarter of the bound: run 2 averaged 5,482 input tokens per call.

**Tests** ([tests/test_input_budget.py](tests/test_input_budget.py), 7 new):
- an oversized request is refused with **nothing sent**;
- a request within the budget is sent once;
- the bound counts bytes rather than characters, and counts the schema twice;
- the setting's default, override and limits;
- the factory passes the budget to the client;
- the default leaves at least 1.5× headroom over the realistic replay;
- a budget too small for any request sends nothing, and the investigation still ends inconclusive with no retries.

All 7 failed before the change; removing the refusal makes the 2 enforcement tests fail.

**Results:** 568 passed, and `git diff --check` is clean.

## Revised cost and risk

| | Calculation | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| Expected | Like runs 1 and 2 ($0.011 and $0.009), plus the wider metrics window | about $0.01–0.02 |
| **Ceiling with the budget** | 16 requests × 64,000 × $0.15/M + 16 × 4,000 × $0.60/M = $0.154 + $0.038 | **$0.20** |
| Previous ceiling (no budget) | Data bound of 143k per request | $0.40 |

**What is and isn't guaranteed:**
- The 16-request, 4,000-output-token, one-investigation and 360 s limits are enforced: 16 requests and 4,000 output tokens by our code (OpenAI enforces the output cap), one investigation by `API_MAX_ACTIVE_INVESTIGATIONS=1`, and 360 s by the server's hard limit.
- The input budget is enforced by our code. The bound on our own text is exact.
- **The one unverified assumption:** that OpenAI's own additions per request (schema rendering and message framing) stay within the allowances, which are twice the schema plus 1,000 tokens.
- **How much room there is:** realistic requests bound at about 36k against the 64k budget. A sent request could only be billed above 64k if OpenAI added more than about 28,000 hidden tokens to it. I have no evidence of that happening, but I can't prove it offline.
- **Verification:** the run's log will show bound against actual tokens for every call.
- **Trade-off:** if the model writes unusually long outputs, a late request could be refused unsent. That costs nothing, but it degrades that agent's result.

So I'd describe $0.20 as a ceiling that holds under that one stated assumption, not an absolute guarantee.

## Outcome

- **Conclusion:** `probable_cause_identified`. The report was model-written.
- **Hypotheses:**
  1. **H1 [high]** "Database Connection Pool Saturation". Supported by the pool at 100% against a 38.4% baseline (direct), and by the config change that cut the pool from 40 to 8 (direct).
  2. **H2 [medium]** "Increased Load on Payments API"
  3. **H3 [low]** "Underlying Database Issues"
  4. **H4 [low]** "Configuration Error in Related Services"
  5. **H5 [low]** "Network Issues Between Services"
- **Decision checks: all 9 passed.**
  - direct support: 3 observations from 2 independent sources (changes, metrics);
  - no material contradiction, no credible alternative, H1 clearly ahead and confident;
  - timeline "ordering ambiguous": the change and the first metric symptom both fall at 09:55;
  - all 3 follow-up queries ran.
- **Critic:** the final verdict was still **"revise"** at the 2-round limit, with 8 unresolved issues. None cites direct evidence against H1; they're about missing support for H2–H5 and missing request-rate detail for H1. The code's adjudication accepted the cause on the evidence, despite the critic's open verdict.
- **Metric baseline:** pool **"100 vs baseline 38.4 (+161%): changed"** and latency "+1253%: changed", both direct. In run 2, 100% was classified "no change" and counted against the right cause.
- **Grounding:**
  - all citations resolved, and all 5 facts stayed facts;
  - code removed the model's duplicate critic round-limit limitation;
  - **not caught:** H2 ("increased load") is supported by a *latency* rise, not request-rate evidence. The request rate was flat, and this structural check can't tell the difference.

## Real-model validation

**Run 4 (`inv-7372f0aaa41c`) completed successfully but did not score correct against the answer key.** It ended inconclusive, so its score is `missed_inconclusive`.

**What worked:**
- The model's top hypothesis was "DB Connection Pool Size Reduced from 40 to 8 by CHG-E01-2" (medium), naming the cause itself.
- The metrics window started at onset, so the baseline fix applied: the fetch was widened to 09:12, and the pool read as "changed vs 38.4%".
- "Increased load" dropped to low, with the flat request rate cited against it.
- The duplicate round-limit limitation was removed.

**Why it stayed inconclusive:**
- **The model's citation error.** It filed the timeout logs as evidence *against* its own leading hypothesis and attached the config change to H2, so the direct-support and contradiction checks failed. Code checks what evidence *is*, not what it *means*, so it can't correct that.
- **A defect from my Phase 2 change, found in this run.** One boundary sample inflated the noise floor, so the error rate's jump from 0.2% to 7.61% read as "normal". I've fixed it and verified the fix on run 4's own evidence. H1 didn't cite that sample, so it didn't decide run 4. No real model has run with this fix yet.

**Measured:** 9 calls (limit 16), 59,987 input and 5,199 output tokens, 74.2 s, about **$0.012**. No request was refused by the budget. The server was stopped and the port closed afterwards.

| Run | Conclusion | Top hypothesis | Answer key |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | inconclusive | Pool saturation due to reduced pool size (high) | missed |
| 2 | inconclusive | Insufficient pool size (low) | missed |
| 3 | **cause identified** | Database Connection Pool Saturation (high) | **wrong conclusion** |
| 4 | inconclusive | Pool size reduced 40→8 by CHG-E01-2 (medium) | missed |

**Every run cited both key evidence items, but none scored correct.**

- Ranked hypotheses, supporting and contradicting evidence, confidence levels, facts versus assumptions, and inconclusive outcomes.

For each requirement, cite the exact file, class/function, test, or documentation section that supports the finding. Mark requirements that have not been verified as unverified rather than assuming they are complete.

### Required evaluation

- At least 10 synthetic incidents.
- Misleading evidence, conflicting observations and tool failures.
- Multi-agent and single-agent baseline using the same tools.
- Root-cause accuracy, evidence grounding, tool-call count, latency and token consumption.
- Fairness and limitations of the comparison.

Explicitly note that four live-model runs on one case did not score correct against the unchanged answer key. Do not describe the multi-agent system as superior to the baseline based on the current results. Distinguish offline evaluation from real-model evaluation.

### Required deliverables

1. Runnable implementation and setup instructions.
2. Architecture diagram and explanation.
3. Synthetic dataset and automated tests.
4. Evaluation results comparing both approaches.
5. Technical note no longer than 500 words.
6. AI coding assistant transcript.

Check the actual files, not just statements in README.

### Required constraints

- No hardcoded answers for individual test cases.
- No polished frontend required.
- Incomplete functionality and external dependencies documented.

Search for suspicious incident-specific hardcoding and report evidence with file/line references. Do not modify code.

## Step 5 — Check the submission package

Determine whether the current repository itself is the intended package or whether a separate submission directory exists.

## Step 6 — Produce the final audit report

Return a concise but thorough report with these sections:
