# Orange Invest — Implement Remaining Evaluation and Accuracy Gaps

Work in the existing Multi-Agent Production Incident Investigation System repository.

The current reviewer-ready commit is `b65d8e363d59bc3afa3da21820ab8c80bbd16d49`. First inspect the actual current HEAD and working tree; do not assume the repository has not changed.

## Objective

Improve the reliability of root-cause investigations and implement a fair evaluation process for the multi-agent system versus the single-agent baseline, following the original Orange Invest assignment.

## Strict constraints

- Do not make paid LLM/API calls in this task.
- Do not push or commit.
- Never read, print, modify or stage `.env` or secrets.
- Preserve all existing live-run artifacts and `SHA256SUMS`.
- Preserve the answer key and existing incident labels.
- Do not hardcode answers for individual evaluation cases.
- Do not make the tests pass by weakening assertions or changing expected answers.
- Do not claim improved real-model accuracy without new, valid real-model measurements.
- Keep the README, SRS, architecture documents and technical note consistent with the actual implementation.
- Inspect existing code before designing new abstractions; reuse working components wherever possible.

## Phase 1 — Audit the remaining accuracy problems

Inspect the current implementation of:

- `app/agents/reasoning.py`
- `app/agents/grounding.py`
- `app/agents/report_rules.py`
- `app/agents/evidence.py`
- `app/agents/adjudication.py`
- `app/baseline/single_agent.py`
- `app/evaluation/`
- `app/orchestration/`
- Existing tests and `docs/LIVE_RUN_EVAL01_ANALYSIS.md`

Identify which safeguards already exist and which are missing. Do not duplicate existing logic.

Use the four recorded EVAL-01 runs as regression evidence. In particular:

1. Run 4 incorrectly attributed timeout logs against its leading hypothesis and attached the configuration change to another hypothesis.
2. Run 3 identified a symptom but did not correctly name the underlying configuration change.
3. A boundary sample distorted the error-rate noise floor.
4. The Reasoning prompt previously contained an example resembling EVAL-01's expected answer. That example has been replaced, but the leak-free prompt has not been validated by a new real-model run.

## Phase 2 — Improve evidence-to-hypothesis verification

Implement or strengthen a verification layer that evaluates evidence against each individual hypothesis.

It should check, where applicable:

- Evidence IDs actually exist in the retrieved evidence.
- The cited service and component match the hypothesis.
- Evidence timestamps are consistent with the incident timeline.
- A configuration change precedes the incident when it is claimed as a cause.
- Metrics support the stated direction and magnitude of a change.
- Contradictory evidence is not silently treated as supporting evidence.
- Symptoms are not automatically presented as root causes.
- Missing or incomplete evidence is not interpreted as proof that a hypothesis is false.
- A configuration change that precedes an incident is a candidate cause, not automatically a proven cause.

Use structured evidence assessments rather than relying solely on free-form LLM explanations.

Distinguish between:
- Deterministic checks that can be reliably enforced by code.
- Semantic judgments that still require model reasoning.

When evidence is ambiguous, preserve uncertainty rather than inventing a deterministic conclusion.

Add tests based on the failure patterns from Runs 3 and 4. Tests must use actual evidence records and realistic counterexamples, not just mocked assertions that a helper returned a desired string.

## Phase 3 — Validate metric baseline correctness

Review the existing noise-floor fix in `app/agents/evidence.py`.

Add regression tests for:

- Incident-period samples excluded from the pre-incident baseline.
- Boundary samples and duplicate timestamps.
- Large error-rate increases against a clean baseline.
- Insufficient historical samples.
- Missing, incomplete and irregularly spaced observations.
- A normal metric that should not be falsely classified as anomalous.

Ensure the same baseline semantics are used by both metric classification and model-facing evidence summaries.

If the existing implementation already satisfies these requirements, retain it and add only missing tests. Do not introduce another baseline algorithm unnecessarily.

## Phase 4 — Verify prompt independence

Review every prompt sent to the model, including all six agents, shared prompt rules, and the single-agent baseline.

Confirm that:
- Expected answers, evaluation labels and incident-specific answer summaries are not included in model inputs.
- The leak-prevention tests cover model-facing prompts, not just source-code comments.
- Evaluation labels are accessed only by the scorer after the investigation result is produced.
- No prompt contains a test-specific workaround.

Retain and strengthen the existing `tests/test_prompt_leakage.py` checks without weakening them.

## Phase 5 — Implement a fair evaluation harness

Inspect the existing evaluator and baseline before adding anything.

Build or improve a reproducible evaluation runner that compares the multi-agent system with the single-agent baseline using:

- The same incident inputs.
- The same evidence tools and evidence availability.
- The same model and model configuration for a live comparison.
- The same answer key, scoring rules and timeout policy.
- The same limits on evidence requests, retries and execution time wherever applicable.

Ensure the answer key and scoring labels are never included in the investigation prompts.

For each approach and incident, record:

- Strict root-cause accuracy.
- Lenient root-cause accuracy.
- Correct, incorrect and inconclusive outcomes.
- Evidence-grounding score.
- Citation validity and unsupported-claim count.
- Tool-call count.
- Latency.
- Input and output token counts for real-model runs.
- Estimated model cost when pricing information is available.
- Errors, timeouts and incomplete evidence.
- Model identifier and relevant configuration.

Report denominators and missing measurements clearly. Do not silently exclude failed or inconclusive investigations.

Support reproducible offline evaluation and a separately enabled live-model evaluation mode. Offline results must be clearly labelled as heuristic-stand-in results, not real-model accuracy.

## Phase 6 — Prepare a safe live evaluation without executing it

Prepare a command or script for a controlled, leak-free real-model comparison.

Requirements:

- Explicit opt-in for billed calls.
- A separate explicit confirmation for the live evaluation command.
- Existing input-token, output-token, timeout, concurrency and request limits.
- A documented maximum budget with a preflight cost estimate where possible.
- A clear abort path on budget or safety guard failures.
- A unique output directory for each evaluation run.
- No overwriting previous results.
- No automatic retry of a complete investigation submission that could cause additional charges.
- Checksummed results and a README recording provenance, configuration, limitations and outcomes.

Use the current supported billing and budget guards. Do not invent a second mechanism that bypasses or weakens them.

Do not execute the live evaluation in this task. Present the exact command, planned number of model requests, cost-estimation method, and any uncertainty in the estimate for my approval first.

## Phase 7 — Update tests and documentation

Add or update tests for every fix

## Why I stopped

The order is already paired by incident ([runner.py:103-104](app/evaluation/runner.py#L103-L104)): each case runs multi-agent, then single-agent. The budget, though, is checked **per run** ([runner.py:105](app/evaluation/runner.py#L105)).

Near the cap, a multi-agent run can start (spend + $0.192 fits) while its single-agent partner can't (spend + $0.144 no longer fits). That incident would then have one result and one `not_run`, and the two approaches would be scored on different incidents.

This is unlikely at the expected spend of about $0.24, but it isn't guaranteed. A run that fails without reporting usage is charged its full $0.192 worst case, which can bring a split forward.

## What must change: a small fix, about 15 lines plus tests

1. **Reserve the budget per incident, for both approaches together.** Start an incident only if the spend so far plus *both* worst cases ($0.336) fits. Otherwise record both of its runs as `not_run`. With $1, incidents start while spend is ≤ $0.664, and at about $0.24 expected, all 10 pairs should fit. A pair can never be split.
2. **Report paired results explicitly.** List the incidents both approaches completed, and compute the comparison over exactly those, alongside the all-planned figures.
3. **Tests:** a budget that would split a pair must skip the whole incident instead, and the paired figures must use only incidents both approaches completed.

Same model, evidence, answer key, scoring and limits for both approaches; no other change.

## One more thing to confirm

You asked me to keep `LLM_ALLOW_BILLED_CALLS=true` in `.env`, and I have. While it's there, **any** server or CLI started from this folder makes billed calls without a per-command opt-in, which is outside this run's budget guard.

**To continue, approve:** implement the fix above with its tests, commit it locally, then run the no-call preflight and the live evaluation with `--max-cost-usd 1 --confirm-billed`. Or, if you accept the small risk of a split pair, approve running the current evaluator as it is.

The tracebacks are **harmless cleanup noise, not failed investigations**. All 13 errors come from `AsyncClient.aclose()`, the HTTP client closing its connection pool. Each investigation runs in its own event loop and gets its own client. When a finished run's client is cleaned up after its loop has closed, Python logs "Event loop is closed". By then the investigation is over and its results are recorded, so this doesn't affect results or cost.

It does tell me something useful: about 13 clients have finished, so the run is progressing through the investigations. It started at 09:04:26 UTC, and the process is still running. Progress isn't otherwise visible, because the evaluator logs only errors.

**What's running:** I'm testing your incident-investigation system on all 10 practice incidents (EVAL-01 to EVAL-10), using the real OpenAI model (gpt-4o-mini). Each incident is solved twice:

1. **Multi-agent system:** the six agents working together. This is your main design.
2. **Single-agent baseline:** one agent doing everything, used for comparison.

That's 20 investigations in total. Both approaches get the same incident, the same evidence and the same limits, so the comparison is fair.

**Why we're doing it:** so far the system has only been tested properly on one incident (EVAL-01, four times). This run measures accuracy on all 10 with the real model, so the submission can report real numbers. The offline scores come from a rule-based stand-in for the model, so they can't be presented as real performance.

**Safety limits in place:**
- **Spending:** a hard cap of $3. A run only starts if the worst case still fits under the cap, and the expected total is about $0.25.
- **Previous results:** nothing is overwritten. This run gets its own new timestamped folder.
- **No retries:** if something fails, it stops safely.
- **Your files and git:** `.env` is untouched, and I won't commit or push.

**What I do when it finishes:**
1. Check that every output file exists and isn't corrupted (checksums).
2. Confirm each incident was run by both approaches.
3. Report how many it got right with each approach (strict and lenient), how often it cited evidence correctly, the time, tokens, actual cost and money left from the $3, and whether the new checking rules finally got EVAL-01 right.
4. List the honest limitations.

I won't claim the system improved unless the numbers actually show it.

### Assistant

The live evaluation finished cleanly at 15:08 local. All 20 investigations ran (10 incidents × 2 approaches), with no errors, no budget stop and no retries. It cost about **$0.13 of the $3 budget**. Only 3 of the 10 incidents were solved outright, so this is not an improved-accuracy result.

## Results

| | Multi-agent (your system) | Single-agent baseline |
|---|---|---|
| **Strict accuracy** (correct answer) | **3/10 (30%)** | 2/10 (20%) |
| **Lenient accuracy** (an acceptable "not sure" also counts) | **5/10 (50%)** | 4/10 (40%) |
| **Confidently wrong answers** | **0/10** | 1/10 (EVAL-03) |
| Real cause ranked as the top suspect | **7/9 (78%)** | 3/9 (33%) |
| Key evidence found | **78%** | 17% |
| Citations pointing at real evidence | 242/242 (100%) | 117/117 (100%) |
| Unsupported supporting citations | 0 of 82 checked | 0 of 29 checked |
| Average time per investigation | 105 s | 26 s |
| Cost | $0.101 | $0.034 |

The top-suspect and key-evidence figures count only the 9 incidents that have a real root cause. EVAL-09 is designed to have no cause.

**What the numbers say:**
- **Multi-agent is ahead on every quality measure.** It found the real cause as its top suspect in 7 of 9 incidents and made no confidently wrong calls.
- **Its main weakness is being too cautious.** In 5 incidents it had the right answer on top but still said "inconclusive" (EVAL-02, 03 and 05). Once more evidence-checking failed (EVAL-06), and once it was fooled by the distractor deployment (EVAL-10). The baseline shows the same pattern, so this is the biggest remaining gap.
- **With 10 cases and one run each, a gap of one or two cases is noise.** "Better on ranking and evidence, slightly better on accuracy" is the claim the data supports, not "clearly more accurate".

## EVAL-01: the new rules changed the outcome

In runs 1–4 it was never correct. This time it is **correct with high confidence**: "Database connection pool size reduced from 40 to 8 by CHG-E01-2", with all 27 citations valid and all key evidence found. That is one run, so it's encouraging but not proof. The baseline still answered "inconclusive" on EVAL-01.

## Checks done

- **Commit and push:** the code is at commit `090c497`. Nothing was pushed, nothing new was committed, and `.env` was not touched.
- **Pairing:** every incident ran multi-agent then baseline, back to back. No pair was split, and nothing was `not_run`.
- **Model:** gpt-4o-mini, with an input limit of 64k tokens, 2 critic rounds, a 300 s deadline and 2 attempts per task.
- **Tokens:** 466k in and 51k out (multi-agent); 163k in and 16k out (baseline).
- **Output:** [evaluation_results/live/20261027T090426Z/](evaluation_results/live/20261027T090426Z/), which holds `report.md`, `results.json`, `per_case.csv`, `README.md` and `SHA256SUMS`. All checksums are OK.
- **Earlier runs:** all four EVAL-01 run folders in `artifacts/live-runs/` still pass their checksums. Nothing was overwritten.

## Limitations

- These are 10 small synthetic cases with one run each, so nothing here is statistically significant.
- Accuracy is scored by keyword matching against the answer key.
- Cost is estimated from reported token usage at standard prices; I haven't checked it against the OpenAI bill.
- The run's README says "with uncommitted changes". The only reason is that the run's own results folder isn't committed yet; there are no code changes.
- The log is full of harmless "Event loop is closed" cleanup messages. They come from the evaluator starting a fresh event loop for each run.
- `LLM_ALLOW_BILLED_CALLS=true` is still set in `.env`, as you asked. That means any future command could make paid calls without that extra opt-in.
- `docs/EVALUATION.md` and the README don't include these live numbers yet.

The results folder is uncommitted. If you approve, I can commit it locally, add these live results to the README and `docs/EVALUATION.md`, or both.

6. Verify the remote contains the final commit.
7. If push fails or the remote is ambiguous, stop and report the exact issue; do not guess or force-push.

Do not amend, reset, or rebase the existing commits. Do not commit `.env` or secrets.

## Phase 6 — Prepare the demo video

Once the repository is ready, inspect the actual application and generate a concise 3–5 minute demo script based on the real implementation.

Include:
- 0:00–0:20 — Project introduction and problem statement.
- 0:20–1:00 — Architecture and the distinct agents.
- 1:00–2:00 — Run one representative incident investigation using the actual application/CLI.
- 2:00–3:00 — Show evidence retrieval, tool failures or misleading evidence, hypothesis ranking, confidence, and the final report.
- 3:00–4:00 — Show the baseline comparison and actual live evaluation results.
- 4:00–5:00 — Explain limitations, reproducibility, and key engineering decisions.

Adapt timings to fit 3–5 minutes. Use only commands and features verified in the repository. If a UI does not exist, demonstrate the CLI or report files instead of inventing a UI.

Give me:
- Exact terminal commands to start the application and run the demo.
- The best incident to demonstrate and why.
- Exact files/screens to show.
- A natural spoken narration, not generic marketing language.
- Any setup steps I must complete before recording.
- A fallback if a live demo fails.
- A short final submission checklist.

Do not record or upload a video unless the available environment explicitly supports it and I ask you to do so.

## Final response format

Return a concise report with:
1. Assignment compliance checklist: PASS / PARTIAL / FAIL / UNVERIFIED.
2. Genuine blockers found and fixes made.
3. Test, lint, checksum, and technical-note results.
4. Actual final commit hash and push verification.
5. Files committed and confirmation `.env` was not staged or changed.
6. Remaining limitations, clearly disclosed.
7. Demo commands and a ready-to-read 3–5 minute narration.
8. Final recommendation: READY TO SUBMIT or NOT READY, with reasons.

Work autonomously through safe local checks and necessary fixes. Ask me only if a real external decision or permission is required.
